rible in every sense of the word. As for adjust- ment, this does not mean keeping the chain round about the right tension. Nearly all chains have a habit of running tight in some places, while remaining loose in others. It is necessary frequently to test the tightness all round as you turn the engine, and so to adjust the chain that there is a slight sag at the tight- est spots. In nearly every case of a broken chain the trouble is-due to the chain being ad- justed after having been tested at one of the loosest spots. The way to clean it is to press a stiff brush dipped in paraffin against the lower run while the engine is running, after every respectable ride. After every 500 miles or so you should dismount it, scrub it in paraffin, and then soak it in melted tallow, which re- lubricates it. Engine oil may be used for lubrication at a pinch. When using the brush I advise you to stick it through a big sheet of cardboard to act as a screen to keep the dirty oil at a respectful distance.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

HOW TO RETREAD A COVER.

Many cycle tyres are discarded long before their life is over. Because the rubber has worn very thin on the tread, the cyolist mistrusts it and rather than risk an endless succession of punctures he usually buys a new tyre. This is poor economy. Any tyre that has merely worn thin and is sound in other respects can be re- treaded, and the job is one that no amateur need hesitate to tackle. Special bands for retreading are quite inexpensive and can be bought at all cycle shops of any standing. The main thing to bear in mind in retreading a tyre is to have both tyre and band absolutely clean. Wash the tyre first in warm water, allow it to dry thoroughly, then clean it with petrol and finally roughen it up all round with a file. Petrol alone is sufficient to clean the band, but do not forget to roughen that, too. Give both band and tyre two coats of good solution, applying it with a brush and working it well in. Leave them alone until the solution is, to all appearance, quite dry—say, a quarter of an hour—then turn the band inside out, lay it on the tyre for half its circumference, turn the remaining half right side out and attach that to the tyre. Until the band is in position all the way round do not trouble about sticking the edges; leave those to the last and then see that they do'stick.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0005.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE THE BY-ROADS SCORE.

Of late years I have become more and more addicted to cycling along the by-ways rather than the highways. There are two chief reasons for this. One is that the main roads are usually overrun by motor cyclists and motor cars; the other is that the surfaces are generally speaking far Vetteeirn the side roads than on the main roads. I succeeded two summers ago, after a great deal of opposition, in getting the secretary of the club to which I belong to plan his fixture list so as to include the less frequented tracks, and I notice from the published lists that many clubs are now following this line. The by-roads very often run parallel to the main roads, but they take in villages and hamlets which are teeming with interest and delightfully old- worldish. Too frequently they are missed by the average rider. To do a hundred miles a day is nothing. To enjoy every moment spent awheel is the main object. Try the side tracks and judge for yourself.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
TO ALIGN A SIDECAR.

A bad alignment plays havoc with the tyres and throws a tremendous strain upon the frame. In spite of this fact, however, it is very com- mon indeed to see a sidecar badly aligned; I don't think it is an exaggertation to say that pretty nearly 50 per -cent. of the outfits one meets on the road are faulty in this respect. To test whether a sidecar is in proper align- ment, first of all see that the wheels are vertical, using a piece of string with a weight at the end for the purpose. If the sidecar wheel and cycle wheel incline towards one another, or away from one another, remedy the defect by altering the couplings. This done, push the out- fit along a wet road or path, with the front wheel locked straight ahead. If the tyre tracks are super-imposed the frame is true. To make assurance doubly sure, especially with a sidecar who's& wheeli fitted. level with the back wheel of the cycle, it is a good plan to measure with a piece of string the distance between the two tyres at, say, Six positions round the tyres.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
A WIND SCREEN DEVICE.

Everybody who has driven a car at all regu- larly must have at one time or another been greatly inconvenienced owing to the rain gather- ing on the wind screen; and thus making it a matter of extreme difficulty to see clearly through the glass. A device to overcome this has been brought out in America. An additional screen is provided attached to the front of the hood. When the hood is folded at the back the screen lies upon it, and is therefore out of the way. When the hood is in use, however, this screen is fastened at an angle of about 45 degrees from the uprig.ht in front of the ordin- ary screen. The idea is novel and yet one that should work extremely well. It is claimed that the original screen is kept dry, and, moreover, that if the screen and hood do not meet, it does away with any draught. In theory it Sounds excellent, but I should like to see one in ppactice. If it does answer it will 'certainly prove a great boon.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK UNDER DIFFICULTIES.

Padgate Cottage Homes Teachers Congratulated By Inspector.

A highly satisfactory report on the work of the industrial teachers at the Padgate Cottage Homes by a Board of Education Inspector was referred to at a 'meeting of the Board of Guard- ians on Wednesday. Certificates of efficiency and competency (both first division) had been awarded Mrs. A. W. Hi'ditch and Miss A. Chadwick.

The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) read an extract from a letter by a Board of Education Inspector who had visited the Padgate Home Schools. He pointed out that the school had been carried on under great difficulties. Though the number of children taught had been reduced by the trans- ference of 60 Roman Catholic children to other institutions, there were still 98 on the roll. The teaching of the 63 older children in stan- dards from I. to VI., though attacked with energy and skill, was beyond the power of any teacher to accomplish satisfactory. The infants were taught by a supplementary teacher, who during an emergency marked all exercises for the mixed departments.

The Inspector pointed out that the inadequate staffing appeared to have arisen owing to the uncertainty as to the date of the opening of a new school in Warrington, to which the older children were to be transferred. As it still appeared doubtful whether this school would be completed before autumn, it was desirable that temporary assistance should, if possible, be obtained at once, and that the position of the certificated teacher, as acting head teacher, should be officially recognised by the Guardians. Both teachers had worked zealously and cheer- fully during the period of shortage of staff, and, in spite of difficulties, had maintained the effi- ciency of the school at a satisfactory level. The Inspector concluded "It may be hoped that their services will not escape notice."

INELIGIBLES' CHANCE.

Handyman Wanted At Padgate Cottage

Homes.

It was reported that the handyman at the Cottage Homes had resigned. It was suggested that in advertising for a successor it should be Stipulated that only men over military age should apply. Mr. Shaw thought the appointment should be open to men ineligible for military service. He wanted .to know if the Guardians would engage n man who had lost an eye through the war, and was suitable for the position. ; The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said he knew of a man who had only one eye, yet he got ten " pos- sibles " out of ten, and was now at the Front. The Chairman described the discussion as being out of order, and it was agreed that only men ineligible for the Army should be con- sidered for the position.

3 One Touch of Nature."

Among some African tribes, when a man pro- fesses his love for a woman and asks her in marriage, she invariably refuses him at first, lest it should appear that she had been thinking of him and was eager to become his wife.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 20, 1916.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Buts.

" But me no buts,' " exclaims a Shake- spearian character; " but " at Bewsey-road Wes- leyan Church an Sunday evening the Rev. A. J. Morton discoursed on "The 'huts' of ,life," and I must needs have a word on the subject. I know not how many years age it must be since I copied from the" Family Friend" these defini- tions of "but": "Hope's extinguisher," "A cloud intercepting the sun's rays," "The herald of disappointment," "A loose keystone, which sometimes brings down the whole fabric," "A blot on the fair page of Anticipation!' The following, by one whom I have never been able to identify, sums the matter up conveni- ently enough:—

Ah ! now ,comes that bitter word of 'but,' Which makes all nothing that was said before. That smoothes andorounds, that strokes and dashes more Than flat denial, or a plain disgrace.' Addenda. _

..___..___ There a& some writers who use the word "but" most unfortunately, employing it in- stead of "and" in really absurd fashion. When- ever a young journalist within my sphere of influence betrayed a tendency that way, it has been My pleasure to correct the habit and nip the evil in the bud. _ _ _ _

Perhaps a generation or so ago this absurd 'conundrum went the rounds: "When is a goat neaily7' the answer being "When it is all 'butt?'" '"ll3ut." I must pass on. War.

After zsacling the war columns in last week's issue, I accidently atumbled lIPOII this remark- able -saying of Montesquieu:: "If Europe should ever be ruined, it will be by its Warriors." 1 have donz had by me :some verses by the Rev. Wm. Crowe on "The Miseries of War:" -Son of a Winchester carpenter, Crowe &Ornately be- came erofessor of IRocitny .and Pußlic Orator, and rector of Alton Barnes—a valuable 'benefice. It strikes one as singular that his poetry never caught the popular ear, for, if I know anything of such literature; some Of . it is •really -very fine —the dines .on war *specially. I -may not here reproduce them in• exteriso, but I venture to commend them to the notice of interested readers. The writer—who flourished 17464825— readily concedes that if the stroke of war fell only on the guilty head ("lf they that make the cause Might taste th' effect, -and .drink them- selves the bitter cup they Mix"), then might even extreme pacifists hdld their peace; 'but. alas! the storm 'beats 'heavier on the exposed innocent, and they that stir its fury stand at safe distance. How true it all is as a description of ambitious and unscrupulous militarism; and yet, after all, how true it remains that in the great crisis of 1914 England could not 'honour- ably or safely hold alocif from what Is summed up in the comprehensive and highly Significant phrase, "this war on war:"

The Farmers' Dilemma. The belligerent countries are threatened with red ruin as well as the breaking up of laws. As to our own still distinctly favoured land, local farmers last week made strong protest against so many strong and -experienced young agriculturists being transferred to the fighting ranks. If the times were normal, there would be much else to be said in their behalf. An increase in the number of agricultural colleges, with fees as reasonable as in, Ireland and Conti- riental countries, might well be urged. And, above and beyond all, security of tenure—that which has enabled the rural classes in Denmark, Holland and France to amass wealth. In the prosperous litle Duchy of Luxemburg, bullied in such heartless fashion by the 'Kaiser, there are 80,000 separate owners farming an average of 71 acres each and working in combination for mutual support and protection through the medium of some 400 co-operative syndicates. Prior to the war I somev/here read that there were in France five and a half million holdings, of which 75 per cent. were owned by the occu- piers. The solution of the land question in Den- mark, which has resulted in almost complete ownership by the farming Class, is co-existent with the prosperity of the people. In Holland 57 per cent. of the occupiers are owners, while 85 per, cent. of the 'farmers occupy lest than, 50 acres of land. As fore Germany, I leave to certain prominent fellow-countrymen the apparently congenial task of exalting and commending the Teuton and all his works; suffice it that, when the wicked cease from troubling and the enemy. regains comparative sanity, legislators urged by reformers should see to it that this country no longer lags so far in the rear of the open-eyed Continentals in this vitally important matter of agriculture on common-sense lines. , Food Values.

-oPe-iiing paragraph in last week's "Home Notes" reminded me of a period some years ago when "diet in the dog days" (and we may at least be said to be tending thitherward) was the subject of numerous articles which attempted to advise the public. , The average man requires from 40 to 60 ounces of so-called solid food, and from 50 to 80 ounces of water, during the course of a day, we were told by one contributor to the discussion, who signed his communication "A Physician." As a matter of fact there were at least two writers over that signature, their letters appearing in different papers. One declared that the cardinal rule for eating in hot weather is not so much to shun altogether par- ticular articles of diet, as to diminish the total quantities of all foods eaten. It may well be supposed that in such weather less food is required, as there is not such a loss of heat from the body. I remember hearing or reading of one of our eminent judges, whose motto in deliver- ing judgment and advice to professional brethren was "Never • give reason, s"; and it is when the writer on food values and so on proceeds to particulars and dogmatises in detail that his troubles begin. Be it understood, therefore, that it is "A Physician" and not I who thus lays down the law—" Eight ounces of meat or ten ounces of fish once a day is quite sufficient in warm weather for any adult doing an ordinary day's work, the rest of the amount lof solids required being made up with vege- tables, fruits, and breads of various kinds. The most nutritious of the vegetables are peas and beans. Among fruits, the banana is very nutritious." Our authority goes on to say that on this diet the amount of bread required is about 18oz. of either white or brown, and that scones, biscuits, and the like- may partly take its place—a right comfortable doctrine, surely. And it must be agreed that, as he further urges, heating elements such as fats and oils should be avoided in the summer months. As to eating less as the sun gains power, I im- agine that some of us would be the better for strict dieting all round and . systematically. Hence, let, me echo Admiral Dewey's admirable saving: "One-third of what we eat enables us to live. The other two-thirds enables the doctor to live." On Anger. Last week I referred to persons of my acquaintance who had sulked with near rela- tives for many long and weary years. I re- call a lecture given by Mr. E. L. Ranson in the course of which he incidentally settled such problems as how to cure anger and how not to be angry. Here is a quotation which may serve: "If you see an angry man in the street, and if you think 'how angry that man i's' you make him worse because you are in- creasing the vibration of his anger cells. What you should do is to turn away and realise that there is no anger in Heaven, and directly you have forgotten about the angry man and the material world instantly lie stops being angry." Simplicity itself, as of course the reader at once perceives. But—" nay," I fancy I hear him say, "but. me no buts," so there most willingly I leave it. Reticence.

:fears and years ago I knew of a gentleman , whose groom was noted for extreme taci- turnity. As they were driving in a distant Ipart of the country, on reaching a particular spot the master broke a long silence with, "John, do you like eggs?" The man' replied with a monosyllabic affirmative, and there the incident ended for the time. Twelve months afterwards, in going over the same ground, the master asked, "How ,do you like them, John?" and the simple and sufficient answer ,was "Boiled, sir." There is a similar story (only more so, as the Irishman said) of two Australian bushmen who occupied the same hut, but worked apart during, the day. One morning Bill said "Jack, did you hear that cow bellowin' last night?" Jack answered in the evening, "How d'ye know it warn't a bull?" he inquired. Forthwith Bill packed up his traps and "got a move on." "Seems to me there's getting to be too much argument here," he sadly observed.

WORK FOR ALL.

Red Cross Nurses To Displace

Male Hospital Orderlies.

DARESBURY DIVISION'S PROGRESS,

The war has enabled the women of the country to show their true and noble characteristics, to be a help and comfort to the men in the fight for liberty and justice, and in no sphere of labour have they excelled more than looking after the welfare of the wounded and alleviat- ing their suffering. Tens of thousands of wounded " Tommies 'can testify to the self- sacrificing work done by women in every station of life, and particularly those attached to the British Red Cress Society. The Daresburr Division, Cheshire Branch, with which Warrington is amalgamated, has done, and is doing an invaluable amount of work in 'training womento fit themselvesierhospitals and other Red Cross work. The progress made in this direction was reported upon at a meeting of the branch held in the Council Chamber, Warring- ton, on Saturday afternoon, when the oppor- tunity was taken to present a number of ladies with badges for efficiency, and certificates for having successfully passed examinations in first aid.

There,was a large gathering of ladies, many of whom were - attired in Red Cross uniforms, adorned with badges and medallions of the Society. Miss Sylvia Broadbent, vice-president of the branch, presided, and the company was honoured with the presence of the Mayor '(Alderman Peter Peacock).

===== 0002601_19160520_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL COMPETITORS PASS.

After welcoming the Mayor, the members of ,the St. John Ambulance Brigade, and expressing •regret at the unavoidable absence of the latter's mew ,president, Mrs. Henry Green.all, the Vice- President went on to refer ,to the recent ex- amination in first-aid. She said the course was taken by Major ' Adams, assisted in practical work by Mr. Baldry, of the St. John Ambulance IBrigade, and the examiner was Dr. Bowden. Thirty-one members entered for the examina- tion. All had, passed, and she desired to con- gratulate them. It was a great satisfaction to the officers of the B.R.C.S. that so many were being trained for ,work in hospitals, and great credit was due to the lecturer and examiner, without whom no detachment could be trained. Since last they met the Detachment •" Cheshire 150" had been enrolled and registered, and many were working in the local hospitals. The personnel of the V.A.D.'s of the Daresbury Division was 126, and now more were qualified to join as a result of this examination. The total number of V.A. Detachments in Cheshire was 76 women and 16 men, and the total personnel for Cheshire was 4,000. The brassards issued numbered 2,882, which meant that that number was actually working. __

===== 0002601_19160520_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
Need For Discipline.

She desired to urge upon members the im- portance of discipline. Did the cadets realise that they were as much under the command of their officers as men, in the Army? She appealed to them to show that women could keep regulations and work in as loyal and methodical a manner as men. The women of England were now on their trial, and it was up to them to prove their w,orth when the oppor- tunity presented itself. She laid stress on the fact that cadets were not at liberty to undertake any work without the permission of their com- mandant. There had been one of two instances

this breach of discipline. Doubtless they were due to ignorance and •carelessness, but ignor- ance should not exist in such matters.

Miss Broadbent went on to refer to the splen- did work done by the cadets, many of whom had been working for nineteen months, and to whom the greatest credit was due for their loyalty and devotion. One of their officers had just been to Devonshire House, the headquarters of the joint committee of the 13.R,C.5. and St. John Ambu- lance, and bad ..heard .there ,Trould be work, for all who proved themselves capable. In addition to the nursing and general service department, the B.R.C.S. had taken over part of the Post Office in France, and had sent an ambulance convoy for women drivers. If this were satis- factory more would be sent (Applause.) Mili- tary hospitals were now required to replace their men orderlies, &c., with women. She had recently visited Orford Barracks, where their Red Cross nurses had been working for over a year and ,where this change had been partially made, and would soon be completed. The result had been most satisfactory, and the women's touch had already brightened the aspect of the wards.

"I don't intend any disparagement to the men—all honour to the men of all ranks in the British Army—but they are needed we know for more active work. We feel proud that our Society can release them." Doubts Would Vanish.

A Registrar of a hospital told her he was dreading the day when he must replace his men with women. -If he had only seen volunteer nurses and paid cleaners working with their Ihearts as well as their hands, his doubts would vanish. (Applause.) Touching upon the financial assistance given their cause, Miss Broadbent said the proceeds of the Royal Court Theatre matinee in aid of the registry working parties resulted in £6.5 us., which had been divided between Stretton and Appleton, Latchford, Whitley, and Grappen- hall. The working parties had done extremely good work, and had contributed Many garments and hospital necessities to the Red Cross Hos- pital and the headquarters depot in London. The recent sale at Appleton, which was a wonderful success, brought in a sum of £75 for the local working party. The next object they hoped to attain was the gift of a motor ambulance to the county for use locally, and to'be retained after the war in Warrington. The need locally was more urgent than elsewhere. Those who sub- scribed to the fund were not only helping dur- ing the war, but afterwards, when it would be used for town casualties. Miss Broadbent announced that Dr. Bower had been made a life honorary member of the 8.R.C.5., in recognition of the many courses of lectures he had given voluntarily; and Oh behalf of the division she offered congratulations to him. She also thanked Dr. Fox for the services he had rendered to the Society, and also for his instruction to members and patients of Raddon Court Hospital in radiography; also Mr. Hedley Taylor, the hon. dental surgeon. Miss Broadbent then called upon the Mayor_to present the following ladies with badges and certificates :

MAYOR'S FIRST-AID LESSON.

A vote ot thanks to the Mayor was proposed by Mrs. Fox and carried. The Mayor, in a brief reply, admitted that he only attended one lecture, on first-aid. When the lecturer showed how to stop bleeding in the ease of anyone having a leg cut off, hel (the Mayor) immediately fainteci over. He did not attend any more lectures as he thought he was not a fit subject. (Laughter.) Ile was pleasel that so many ladies had obtained certificates. The work of the Red Cross Society needed no words of commendation from him; its noble work was well known. But for the Red Cross Society the men of England would have been in a sorry plight.

LARGE AND SMALL

Unseparated Potatoes Mean

Thousands Of Tons More.

FARMERS' NEW STEP.

Urged by the Board of Agriculture to pro- duc9 as much food as possible, Warrington and District Farmers, at a meeting held at the Blue Bell Inn, Horsemarket-street, on Wednesday evening, embarked upon a departure from ordinary custom which, it was claimed, will be a mutual benefit to themselves and to the com- munity. It relates to the supply of-early potatoes, and by the new method of lifting them, it is antici- pated that the supply in June will be enhanced by several thousand tons in this district alone. With the high prices reigning owing to the present great shortage, this will be regarded as particularly good news. for the consumer. _ _

The Chairman of the meeting, Mr. Edward Marsh, of Bradley Hall, said that under the abnormal circumstances at present existing, it was essential that they should get their early potatoes into the market .as soon' as possible. But this would be an awkward matter. if they had to discard the little from the big ones, as the labour of doing. this was tedious, laborious and expensive. It was much quicker to use a digger drawn by a horse, this machine throwing the potatoes out on the top of the furrows and leaving the women and children to gather/ them. Under the system usually fol- lowed, a great deal of expense was involved in separating the smaller from the larger potatoes. All that would be done away with if they unanimously agreed to send to the market the whole lot as one sample. Their proposal was pot being suggested in order to take any mean advantage of the general public. There was no intention to do-anything of the kind. All that they wanted was to get their crop of early potatoes to the market as soon as possible in order to relieve what was at present a very grave situation as regarded the scarcity of 'p'otatOes, now being sold at an abnormal price. He con- Sidered that the action was the most loyal they could .10. for then they would relieve the ten- sidn of the food market.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
BENEFIT FOR ALL.

Mr Itugh Derbyshire, of Stretton, who was the first promoter of the scheme' in this district, .consiitered that it Would be beneficial both for the farmer and for the consumer, for, in the first ptace, f -inners had very few waggonus left, and thc:e I.P-unlined mainly only wom %n and cliii- dcon to de the work. By work;ng. together; however, farmers could put their early potatoes into the market as one sample, thus saving a considerable amount of labour. •If they all stood together, merchants would have no choice, but would have to take what was placed before them. He suggested that farmers i a the various districts represented, should hold meetings and urge upon all producers only to send one sample into the market. The Board of Agriculture as urging them week by week to grow as much food stuff as possible, and if they could save them- selves the labour of 'separating the potatoes, they would be meeting the spirit of the authorities. Mr. Joseph T. Baxter, of Dutton Hall, was quite certain that if all the potatoes were placed into the market together there would be no detriment to the community. In Staffordshire this method was adopted considerably, many tons of unseparated potatoes being sent into Wolverhampton and Birmingham. and more- over, there was a great demand for them by greengrocers. If it could be done in one dis- trict, why could It not be done in Warrington? It was no detriment to the public in any shape or form, and certainly it would be a benefit to the farming class. _ _ _

Mr. W. Noreott. of Bold, asked whether it would he possible to ask the " Ormskirk side" to net similarly. The Chairman: It is the idea to get all dis- tricts to do it.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
NO SELFISHNESS.

Mr. Baxter again rose and said he wanted it to be quite clearly understood that the suggestion had not been adopted merely from any selfish point of view. It was advantageous to the pub- lic inasmuch that they would get a quicker and more regular supply. Alderman Bennett said the absolute existence of the State and its welfare depended more upon agriculture than upon any-other industry. The question of labour was a tremendous problem for them, and unity, such as they were now dis- cussing, was the best thing that could be done. It was gratifying to know that buyers or potatoes- would view the proposal with success. As Chairman of the London Bridge Potato Exchange, Mr. Richard Billington, of Stretton, said he felt sure that if they brought that move. ment forward, it would be carried out by their members.

Several farmers from all over the district, in- cluding Culcheth, Knutsford and Northwich, having supported the idea, Mr. Joha Whitlow (Appleton) formally proposed, and Mr. Richard Clark (Warburton) seconded the adoption of the scheme indicated. A strong committee was elected to see that the scheme is properly carried out. it members being: Messrs. E. Marsh, H. Darbyshire, J. Whitlow (Appleton). J. Whitlow (Preston Brook), J. Lowe. A. Mclntyre. John Warburton A. Broome, T. Baxter, W. Norcott and R. Bill- ington.

He thought it was the only class where 100 per cent. of passes had been obtained. Mrs. H. V. It was up to them to get their friends to form classes and so help to relieve the male staffs at the various hospitals.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

WHY FARMERS DO NOT PLOUGH UP

GRASS.

It is not difficult to explain or even justify the hesitation to plough up grass. The crop- ping of the land to-day represents the form of cultivation which the slump of the period of 1879-90 rendered necessary. Farmers may show an excess of natural caution in making changes, but they have burnt their fingers once, and burnt them badly. Some may be waiting till the rise' in the price of foreign wheat has be- come more pronounced and more firmly estab- lished; others may be reluctant to plough up grass which after 25 years has become useful; but all are profoundly distrustful of the politi- cal tendencies of the time and hesitate to em- bark on additional outlay. Those who at the present, for one reason or another, advocate a large immediate addition to the arable area of the country will be wise to remember three points in the farmers' position. The first is that they desire nothing so much as to be left alone. However galling may be their griev- ances, they prefer them to legislative interfer- ence. The second is that they know the natural protection of their industry by the rise in world-prices—without any conditions as to minimum wages or Land Courts—cannot long be postponed. It must come, and come within the next few years. War may disturb, it can- not counteract, the tendency of prices to rise. The third is that they expect a heavy slump in wheat prices after the declaration of peace. When the belligerents—whether allies or enemies—have- resumed their protective policy the exportable wheat surplus of the world will be sold in this country, under Free Trade, by producers who will be ready to take unremun- erative prices rather than not sell at all. With that prospect before them and their experience 'behind them, farmers will not plough grass for wheat unless it is made worth their while.— "Times."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
BACKWARD, BUT SATISFACTORY.

The Crop Reporters of the Board of Agricul- ture, in commenting on agricultural conditions in England and Wales on the Ist May, report that the weather of April, although cold for most of the time, was generally favourable to agriculture, especially the last few days. All 'wheat improved during the month, and that on the lighter lands generally looks well; wheat on low-lying or heavy land, and that which was sown late, is, however, poor generally. Much progress was made with the sowing of spring corn, although a considerable area still remains to be sown. Owing-to the backward season, and. consequent pressure of arrears, there is gener- ally a poor seed bed (except in the north); nevertheless, the young barley and oats, where showing, appear to be a full plant and 'satis- factory. Beans are generally doing well, and appear to be the best of the corn crops in some districts. Potato planting is very behindhand; it is now in full swing in the most important counties, but the bulk of this work was only begun in the last ten days of the month, and a large area still rethains to be planted. Prac- tically no mangels have been sown yet, and in many cases preparation of the land has not yet been done for this crop, owing to the lateness of the corn-sowing season. Fruit trees every- where show abundance of blossom, but they are very late. •

===== 0002601_19160520_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PIG.

The following pithy notes on pig-keeping are published in a pamphlet issued by the St. Edmundsbury Corporation Bacon Factory For a profitable sow, lay more stress on good bone, good constitution, and big litters than on a number in the herd book. Beware of the scrub thoroughbred. Blood without quality is worse than quality without blood. _ . .

The best breed is the one that will rear most pigs and make the most and best bacon on the cheapest food. Save the best sows for breeders. Do sot treed young, immature sows. Do not kill good breed- ing stock too early. Seep a record of the per- formance of each sow. Fatten the rattle-headed sow that lies on her pigs. Try ,another. • When ,pigs are low in Price it is the time to increase the number of breeders. • Avoid scours by keeping things clean about troughs and swill tubs. Aim for a daily gain of one-and-a-half pounds per pig. If you do not work for it, you will probably not get it. A wet pen will make a lame hog. The hog is not responsible for a poor result. A pig does not merely'eat to liv'e. Study the difference between a growing ration and a fattening ration. Pigs consume two pounds of water with every pound of corn—if they can get the water. The cheapest kind of experience is other people's experience. It sounds contradictory, but it is good advice to fatten the hogs lean. There is nothing more convincing than suc- cess, but even success can be improved upon. Quickly grown hogs are by any odds the most profitable. Fatten a poor word. Grow is better. Be merciful even toward the pig about to be killed. The hog is a machine for converting golden corn into golden coin. Put the hogs on the platform scales occasion- ally. You will learn something.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

YOUNG RHUBARB PLANTATIONS.

When newly planted rhubarb is growing strongly many people are tempted to pull some of the stalks. Thig- is a mistake, as doing so checks and weaken the crowns. The better plan is to leave all the stalks this year, and, unless extra strong, even the second season, as the fewer stalks removed the stronger will the various clumps be in after years. It is also advisable in the case of newly formed planta- tions of rhubarb to put down a good mulch of rotten manure between the lines in order to minimise the effects of di'y weather.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
SAVOYS.

These are all too often sown too early, with yie result that they turn in during the autumn and are ready for use long before they are re- quired. Too much attention has been paid to the raising of early varieties which are not wanted, as they often come into use with cauli- flowers, early Brussels sprouts, cabbages, and coleworts, as well as late runner beans, marrows, and other vegetables. It is well known that the flavour of savoys is considerably improved by moderate frost. If however, the heads are too far advanced they, when frosted, are strong and in many cases decay.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SWEET WILLIAMS.

These are among the best of early summer flowers, the self coloured varieties being in every way preferable to those spotted or undecided in colour. Unfortunately .size of individual bloom is all too often thought necessary, as if the in- terest and beauty of a flower were dependent on the breadth of the petals. We like the 'flowers that are of a pronounced colour, as dqep crim- son set off it may be with a white margin or with a white eye, or best of all without any contrast- ing colour—merely self crimson.

FRUIT TREES IN THE KITCHEN GARDEN. che old plan of mixing fruit and vegetables up together ends in both being unsatisfactory, as it is impossible to grow two crops to perfection on the sane piece of ground. Far better de- vote one portion to fruit and the other entirely to vegetables. Fruit trees need all the food they can get from the soil for several yards round their base, if good crops are looked for, and for this reason the wide spreading standard should always be relegated to the orchard proper. Of course many owners halt no room for this arrangement, and are compelled to get both fruit and vegetables from the kitchen gar- den, and in order to do this some restricted form of fruit tree training must be adopted. Here the espalier form of training comes in handy, as trees grown in this way can be grown alongside the walks and will bear heavy crops of fruit if they are given plenty of good soil for the roots to work in, and attention in the way of frequent supplies of liquid manure or Mulch- ings of manure when the trees are perfecting 'their crop. Another advantage in growing espaliers is that it is very easy, should frost come when the trees are in bloom, to protect them in some. way.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY FOR PROFIT.

IMPORTANCE OF GREEN FOOD.

In winter and early spring, as also in the height of a dry summer, the suburban poultry- keeper is apt to stint his poultry of what is. known as "green food." Under this name poul- try-keepers 'group all fresh vegetable growth palatable to fowls, and also certain roots such as- mangels, turnips, carrots, which may be stored or allowed to stand over winter and fed in an undried state. Such preparations as alfalfa an& clover meals and beet pulp whilst not really green food, after steaming partake largely of the nature of fresh vegetables and form a usefut though scarcely sufficient substitute. Green food serves poultry as an actual food- stuff to some extent, but it is chiefly valuable. for adding bulk to the ration, thereby assisting; the intestines in their action. It also possesess valuable tonic properties. The necessary bulk- may be supplied in the form of fibre as when, bran or clover meal is fed, or it may consist' more largely of water as is the case where man- gels are utilised. Since an egg is composed to' a considerable extent of water, it is obvious that the requisite bulk, which the hen seeks by in-- stinct, should be presented to her its far as pos- sible in the form of water. She will then be' better equipped for laying than if she is allowed' to satisfy' herself with dry fibre, from which moreover her digestive system is unable to ex- tract much nutriment. It is this question of supplying water that makes steamed bran or clover meal more useful for egg production than the dry feeds,, since mere steaming has little effect on digestibility.

The Provision of Green ' Food.

Where an adequate grass run is provided the suburban poultry-keeper usually considers that the green food problem is settled. This is so during the months of growth, but dry grass (for example in the heigut of summer) although capable of supplying the necessary bulk to the ration does ea largely in the form of fibre. Some variety of succulent vegetable will give better results. Sprouted oats have been mentioned _ already in this connection, but at the present time of the year provision may be made-in a less expensive manner. Where the double-run system is in use both runs will now be bare unless land is very plentiful. To tide over the 41. time that must elapse before the March-sown crop is ready, a few beds of seeds may be sown under protection in the run occupied by the birds.

If fowls have been kept short of green food and are turned into a fresh crop they will cer- tainly eat more than is good for them. Diarrhma follows, and the flavour of the eggs also suffers. Poultry accustomed to the free run of vege- tables will eat only the amount necessary for them.—r" Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
CHICKENS AND COMING MEAT SHORTAGE.

The season is getting on, for when we get to May everyone looks for brighter weather. Doctors say that ,May is the most trying month of the year, because of the new growth when Nature springs into life and puts on her summer garment. Certain it is that the springtime causes our old folk to seek extra care and not be too venturesome the first warm day. We have had a few fine warm days when the overcoat could be left at home, but since then wet and cold winds, which remind us that winter is not long past. For the chicken-raiser the month of May is very welcome. He expects the cold winds to have gone for the season, and looks ahead with pleasure and comfort to his. stock roaming the fields free from all the biting blasts of March and the early April showers. The early broods must be gearly adult by this time, for there were many dhickens out in January, and if these have been done well they will show the result of that treatment,

• Not for many years have these birds come through such a trying time. The snows and winds have tried birds of all ages, but nothing more than the young chicks which had just begun to see life. Most of them have come through admirably, and are now growing fast. For all young stock there is no time like April and May, because the weather' is more genial,. and the young shoots in the fields keep them in good health and this greatly assists growth. Some of the early birds will be ready for kill- ing, and those forward enough should be moved on as soon as possible. There is no necessity for wasting food over early cockerels which are not intended to be kept on for future breeding. The sooner they go the better, and thus save expense and have more room for the other stock. Look round for a market and clear out with all speed. The money in hand is better than the stock, because they cannot give any return if kept over. Only the other day I heard a butcher express the opinion that we should have meat tickets in England before long owing to the great short- age of meat. If this should be so the poultry- keeper must get a pull, for anything which he is not wanting for himself will find a ready sale, and at remunerative prices. With such a pros- pect in view it becomes necessary for everyone who has any room at all to take up the hatch- ing and raising of a few chicks, if only to find some portion of flesh food for his own use.— "Feathered World."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THIS WEEK'S „WORK IN BRIEF.

The chicks intended for stock should be carefully examined. Birds which may be de- formed or mismarked should be set apart and fattened for the table. None but the best are good enough to breed from. Let the diet be bone forming and varied. A plentiful supply of green food is an absolute necessity just now. Keep the grass in the runs very short. When the culls are about 31b. in weight they should be separated from the rest of the stock and fattened. The most profitable weight to dispose of chickens for table pur- pose is between 31 and 41b. The ventilators may now be left more open at night.—" Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
SELLING EGGS BY WEIGHT.

In country districts the small egg is of equal value to the large one, says "Poultry World." Therefore no inducement is offered the poultry- keeper to- keep large egg laying strains. Ob- viously a large egg contains more food thans does- a small -one; and it is but fair that it should command a higher price. This matter can only be righted by selling eggs by weight. The laying competitions have proved that large eggs can be produced with- out affecting the quantity. Therefore, to offer standard value would be advantageous to both producer and purchaser. Very few breeds which lay small eggs are popular to-day. It is probable that in the near future the large egg will be valued at its. true worth.

' r ettas tally (Ehitor

OUR ATTITUDE TOWARDS GERIVLiNY.

Sir,—ln considering our attitude towards our enemies while being suitably severe we need not be vindictive. The crime of Germany and . .

her Allies -against civilisation is most certainly • a great one, and must be fought to a finish. No half measures will do; we must see that it shall be impossible to renew anything like the Present struggle for the next 50 years at least. The cost of the war has been enormous, and the world will at its close be very poor indeed; and the German Allies ought not to be allowed to escape responsibility in paying a substantial indemnity for the loss and expenditure she has caused by her insane adventure to conquer the world. Our freedom of trade has been exploited for the benefit of our enemies, to provide them With an exceptional fulcrum from which to tap our national resources. In the words of Mr. • Rughes, "we must pluck out German inflttence by the roots, wherever it is, and at all costs, regarding everything as subordinate to this one thing, and we must by settled determination, clearly expressed to the world, decide whatever comes or goes to destroy German control of British trade." As Mr. Runciman told Mr. Forrest, "Peaceful preparation as a means to a military end can never again be tolerated by

England, France, Italy, and Russia, nor can we submit to most-favoured nation' clauses in commercial treaties being utilised to the detri- ment of any one of the Allied countries." "Charity begins at home," and it is our duty _ _ _ _

first to ourselves and the daughter and subject nations which form what is known as the Brit- ish Empire; next come our Allies and the other friendly nations who have stood patiently by during this Titanic struggle; last come the enemy nations who have done their utmost to make the condition of the world unbearable. If there is to be a Union of Friendly States in Europe it certainly cannot include the enemy States until they have had sufficient time to' demonstrate the fact that they have clearly Purged themselves of their past delinquencies— that they have abandoned Bismarckism and Prussian Junkerdom. Above all other aspirations'it must be placed beyond the shadow of a doubt that Britain's sons have not died in vain, and that for the treasure wasted, but more especially for the blood shed, the world shall have progressed on lines of true brotherhood and humanity. The Germans, in their wild adventure of social and Political aggrandisement during recent history, have stopped at nothing to gain their ends. It has been instilled into the people of Germany from the cradle to the grave that by spreading themselves over the whole earth and prying into and exploiting the affairs of their neigh- bours was a patriotic duty, that spying and Patriotism are synonymous terms. Nothing seems to have been too low or mean for them. The talent and ability of men of other nation- alities have been exploited; they have been en- ticed to Germany, there to give of their best, and When all had been got from them, to be returned home or to be made prisoners of War. If peace is even only relatively to be kept in the future these things should not be. We cannot have peace so long as this German old man of the sea" clings to the shoulders of the "Sinbad "of the European nations. It will have to be cast off, and all that is fair, honest and true, not only in Europe, but throughout the civilised parts of 'the world, must combine to put it down. It is not so much Germany; it is not so much German militarism; but it is that spirit of the age which stands behind all things German that has to be combated. Sc) long as that spirit reigns, so long must Ger- many be regarded as a pariah among the nations, despised, detested, and hated by all. When she returns to that saner Germany of the past, willing to live and let live, and be in harmony and peace with those around her, then , she may be welcomed to the European com- munity of nations. Until then it must be other- wise.

PHIL SQUOD.

TWENTY-FIVE YEARS.

Ex-Curate Of St. Peter's Church On

Past Opportunities.

Special music, rendered by the school children and choir, was the chief feature in connection with the school anniversary services, held at St. Peter's Church on Sunday. Special hymns were sung in a fine manner at each service, while in the evening an anthem— "Make a Joyful Noise "—was rendered. Mr. E. A. Padmore presided at the organ. In the afternoon the Young Women's Bible Class delighted the congregation with a render- ing of a service of song entitled "Her Mother's Book." Mrs. Martin accompanied, and Mrs. Percival was the reader. The special preachers for the day were the Rev. S. G. Stanton and the Rev. H. Buckton (a former curate at St. Peter's). Both gentlemen delivered thoughtful and interesting sermons, and each had a word of advice for the children, upon whom, they said, depended the future of the nation. The Rev. H. Buckton added that he was at the laying of the foundation stone of the church, and often went to the old missidn room. In looking back upon those twenty-five years, the question often came to him "Had they made the best of their opportunities ?" He would particularly urge the young people to make the best possible use of their opportunities. During the past quarter of a century their Vicar had worked manfully amongst them, and he was sure the people had backed him up and encour- aged him in everjr possible way.

WALKING DAY.

Arrangements For The Festival Are Well Received.

TWO MILITARY BANDS ENGAGED.

The scheme outlined at the meeting called by the Mayor (Alderman Peacock) last week for the holding of Walking Day this year has met with approval throughout the town, and it is expected that the festival will take place upon the lines then laid down. That is, there will be a general demonstration in which all the schools will take part, and there will be no excursions out of town.

It will be remembered that his Worship undertook to endeavour to solve the band diffi- culty 4 and he has made a very good start to- wards solving that difficulty. He has been in communication With Colonel Layton, command- ing the 3/4th South Lancashire Regiment„ and succeeded in getting that officer's consent to the visit to Warrington for the purpose of assisting in the Walking Day festivities of the South Laneashire Reserve Brass Band and the Bugle Band.

These bands, 70 strong will arrive in War- rington on the Thursday before Walking Day. His Worship has thug killed three birds with one stone—secured much,needed music for the children's day; secured an agreeable hoirlay for a large body of men, who will be pleased to be at home on such an occasion; and made arrangements that will do the battalion good from a recruiting_point af view.

FOR BLINDED HEROES.

Great Hippodrome Matinee Gets Strong Support.

In the afternoon of Thursday, June Ist, the doors of the Palace and Hippodrome will b 3 thrown open to admit a tremendous audience, who will enter to witness a matinee in aid of the Blinded Soldiers' and Sailors' Hostel. That the audience will be tremendous there can be no doubt, for the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Mr. W. A. Jackson (manager of the Hippodrome) and the Working Men's Infirmary Committee are all going to devote their organ- ising ability to making the affair a success. This was decided upon at a meeting of the committee at the Town Hall on Monday even- ing, when the Mayor and Mr. Jackson looked in to appeal to the committee for their help. The Mayor having explained the urgent need of our blinded heroes, Mr. Jackson said that the majority of the music halls had agreed that at an early date the proceeds of a matinee per- formance should be devoted to these men. They wanted to make the undertaking a gigantic suc- cess in Warrington, and his proprietors were pre.. pared to give the theatre. He knew the artistes would give their services free, and also the orchestra, so that as far as the expenses were concerned there would be none, and the entire proceeds would go to that deserving fund. With the increased prices, he thought they ought to get at least £7O. To make it a gigantic success, however, they wanted ticket sellers, and he was assured thaethe Working Men's Infirmary-. Co- mmittee were adepts in this art. He and the Mayor, therefore, appealed to them to do their best. The cause needed little recommendation. Only last week Mr. Macnaghten was in South- ampton, where he heard of a case of a man who had no legs, no arms, and no eyesight. Such cases would enlist the sympathy of all. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19160520_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
COMMITTEE ENTHUSIASTIC.

That the committee desired at once to lend their aid was-made manifest by several speakers, and, upon a resolution being moved and seconded, they were heartily in agreement to do their best for the success of the matinee. Some discussion took place as to whether June Ist was the best day, some members thinking Saturday would be most convenient for them. The difficulties of a Saturday in the near future, however, were insurmountable, and Thursday, June Ist was eventually fixed upon, Mr. Jackson remarking "On that day at any rate, we can get the shopkeepers and, if 'working men gener- ally cannot get, they are sure to be good enough to send their wives." (Hear, hear, and laugh- ter.) In returning thanks to the committee for their ready acquiescence, the Mayor said they were very much obliged to them, for he recog- nised that they had nearly all their work cut out in connection with the Infirmary. Also they all ought to be glad that the Hippodrome management was taking an active part in help- ing charitable objects.

The matinee will be under the patronage of his Worship the Mayor, Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Sir Arthur Crosfield. Bart., and Colonel Moggridge. A conference of ladies was held yesterday afternoon at the Town Hall to make arrange- ments for the diatributicra of tickets* 010 selling of programmes, dm. . .

WOMAN'S WORLD.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Anzac Women.

Everyone knows that the Australian, New Zealand and Canadian troops have covered themselves with glory in the Great War, whilst their womenfolk have also done more than their "bit," and put forth all their energies to help their men and their country. From Australia we learn that the girls in the country districts are taking the place of labourers on the farms, under the direction of the National Land Council. At first the far- mers were far from enthusiastic over the innovation, but soon changed their opinions, and are now only too glad to get the women. British officers, too, home from the Fronts where Dominion troops are stationed, speak highly. of the skill, courage and effi- ciency of Australian, New Zealand and Cana- dian nurses. A great number of Australian women followed their menfolk to Egypt, and many of them are still in Cairo, which has had a very gay winter season in spite of the war. Women's Pay.

It has been urged that a man ought to re- ceive a higher wage than a woman because htt has others dependent on him. In order to find out how many women workers supported relatives, the Women's Group of the Fabian Society have been making special inquiries among various classes, and reports prove that in the teaching profession alone the percen- tage is 49.1. Large numbers of women, it appears, have the same obligations as men in this respect. After the war the proportion will be increased rather than otherwise, and the question of payment may become still. more acute. The problem is a difficult one, and so far no satisfactory solution has been effected.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Roasting Potatoes in Ashes. The best form for cooking the potato is to roast it in the ashes. Potatoes for roasting should be washed and dried carefully, and wrapped in tissue paper and packed in ashes (or sand), then covered with coals and roasted until tender—usually about an hour, depending upon the size of the potato. Next to roasting comes baking—that method of preparing food which may be said to be the result of man's having left the open to live in houses. The oven should be moderately hot and the potatoes should be washed and dried, selected as nearly as possible about the same size and given full possession of the oven. If anything elsc is put into the oven at the same time, they require a longer time to cook and are not so good. Care shoutd be taken to scrub the skins of potatoes to be baked or roasted, as many persons like to eat the roasted skin. Boiling potatoes with their jackets on results in a waste of about three per cent. of their nutritive value, while peeling them before boiling results in about fourteen per cent. loss. Therefore roasting and baking should be more used.

Nuremburg Gingerbread. Take 10oz. flour, 60z. butter, Soz. castor sugar, 4oz. mixed peel, 4 eggs, 6oz. sweet almonds, 2 tablespoonfuls cream, Oz. carbonate of soda, toz. ground ginger, 4 teaspoonful ground cinnamon, teaspoonful ground cloves, a pinch of salt. Sieve the flour, sugar, salt, and spices into A basin, and rub in the butter until free from lumps. Then add the soda, almonds finely shread, and the peel chopped. Beat the eggs and cream together, add them to the dry ingredients, and mix all together. Beat for ten minutes, and half fill greased tins with the mixture. Bake in a moderate oven until the gingerbread is well risen, and feels firm to the touch. Time to bake, 1 to 14 hours if one cake. Probable cost, is. 10(1. Two Nice Cakes.

SUNSHINE FOR WOUNDED SOLDIERS.

My Dear Children,— Your replies on the question of how to bring sunshine to wounded soldiers have shown con- clusively that you have been taking a deep in- terest in the doings of our local hospitals. Our wounded heroes are being very well looked after, but they would fare even better could they be treated, as a whole, in the manner my nephews and nieces would have them treated. Niece Lucy Hankinson, 393, Wellfield-street, who attends Bt. Barnabas' School; wins the prize this week. She is to be congratulated on being successful among so many excellent essays. The bright sunshine of the present week, fol- lowing upon the gentle rain which had preceded it, will bring flowers out rapidly and, as most of my nephews and nieces love flowers and enjoy drawing them, we will have the following as a competition this week:— Draw a Flower or Collection of Flowers. of any kind. I think that will be a popular competition. Don't paint the drawings.

Your loving UNCLE JACK.

ought to have said I have had a letter from Uncle Ned this week. He sends his love to you all and says how glad he is to see the Corner booming. What a Corner he will have when he returhs from active service! You will see that the Corner is now only three short of being 800 strong. Who *ill have the distinction of being the 800th, or of sending in the name of the 800th?

THE WINNING ESSAY. By Lucy Hankinson (784), 393, Wellfield-street ; St. Barnabas' School :--

" How to Bring Sunshine to Wounded Soldiers."

"A wounded soldier needs. every care that human nature can give, because when ill neglect is more easily noticed. If a few wounded soldiers were taken for a drive in a motor car end then taken to a homely cottage, it would remind them of their homes and cheer then up. But if a soldier is bedfast, a visitor to cheer and talk to, or a book to read, would pass away a few monotonous hours. When visiting' the hospitals a few luxuries such as tobacco, cigarettes, fruit,—&c., could be taken and given to the wounded Tommies.' Entertainment at ospitals are allowed, and perhaps a few young soldiers who are interested in football or cricket, would like to watch or referee in some matches. To correspond with the relatives of the wounded 'soldier who cannot write would be a good idea, as the soldiers are eager for news from home. Also to remind the wounded soldier how people will look up to him as a hero, and how he will in 'years 'to come relate the battle .to wandering children of how he was wounded, will cheer

A New Women's Hospital.

The almost completed hospital on Clapham Common will remain a lasting tribute to the energies of British 'medical women of the first two decades of the twentieth century. It will be staffed entirely by women—physicians, chemists, dispensers are all of the gentler sex. Research work will be given special ,considera- tion, and it is hoped that the medical women at this hospital will be induced to specialise in mental diseases. Medical men have, in the past as well as in the present, sadly neglected this branch of medical science with the result that very often the mental sufferer is allowed to degenerate into a state of chronic insanity. The Pereira Medal.

Mi'ss Ella Caird, of Finchley, has this year won the coveted Pereira medal, the highest possible award for pharmacists:. This dis- tinction fell to the lot of a woman in 1908, when Miss Gertrude Holland Wren won it. Miss Ella Caird, however, has beaten all re- cords, for, in addition to the Pereira, she has carried off the silver medal of the Pharma- ceutical Society in botany, chemistry and materia medica. As she is quite young, her achievements are the more remarkable, and her career, which has begun so brilliantly, will doubtless not disappoint her many friends and admirers. The ancient city of Colchester has also made a record during the last week, for it has appointed a woman as Town Sergeant. The lady elected is Miss Ruth Davis, and some modifications of the duties of the post have been made in order to adapt the work to the new official.

Fashionable Shoulder Draperies. A pretty idea is the summer-like finish given to this season's short ostrich neck ruffle. Long tulle ends or streamers are attached to the ruffle, and weighted by means of a tassel, usually of a contrasting colour. Thus black streamers may have an oxidised silver tassel, or -a, ruffle in tete-de-negre one of dull or

Spread only between layers and frost the top with plain frosting. For a "Jenny Lind cake," take 2 cupfuls of sugar, 1 cupful of butter, a cupful of milk, 3 cupfuls of flour, the whites of 6 eggs, 2 teaspoonfuls of baking powder. .Bake two-thirds of the mixture in two long layers, and to the other third add half a cupful of raisins, 2 tablespoonfuls of treacle, 1 teaspoonful of cinnamon, half a tea- spoonful each of cloves and allspice, half a nutmeg. Add a trifle more flour and half a tea- spoonful of baking powder. Place the fruit 'layer between the two white ones, and frost with plain frosting.

HERE AND THERE.

A Hair-Raising Adventure. Small Sister (politely): "I am afraid it will be some time before sister will be down." Suitor (anxiously): "Isn't she well?" Small Sister: "Oh, she's well enough, but Tommy hid the rat for her hair, and it was the longest time before she could find it." Suitor (smiling): "But you say she found it?" Small Sister: "Yes; but Tommy hid her hair, too, and she is looking for that now."

Nothing Doing. "I suppose," said the poor but otherwise truthful young man, cautiously feeling his way, "that you wouldn't be satisfied with love in a cottage?"

"Why not?" queried the fair maid. "I'm sure I'd rather marry the right man with an income of only £l,OOO a year than a million- aire I didn't love."

OTHER PRAISEWORTHY LETTERS.

Niece Lilian Himsworth, 15, Fitzherbert- street (Beamont School).—" How pleasant and welcome is the sunshine to the wounded Tommies ' who have gone surough so much pain for the sake of the Motherland. Love and happiness will do much to keep them bright and cheerful, and also help to restore them to health more quickly. Visiting them or taking drives is a delight, but in the case of the latter we cannot all do. A wounded soldier's face often lights up with pleasure when a gift of cigarettes or fruit is given him. Some of the soldiers we see hobbling with crutches I am sure are lonely, and are longing for a. bit of sunshine, which we can supply them with by kind words and deeds." " P.S.—What a lovely flower it was that won the prize !" Niece Phyllis Lightfoot, 4, Dudley-street (Beamont School).—" I think to bring sunshine and -happiness to our wounded soldiers is to take them, when in hospital, plenty of fruit and flowers; also cigarettes and sweets. They have very good food which the doctor orders for them to do them good." Niece Dorothy Wright, 68, Winwick-street (Wycliffe School).—" Sunshine can be brought to our noble heroes in several ways. First of all I must toll you that they brought a great many wounded soldiers through last week, and I am glad to say although many of them were badly wounded, they were cheered up by the children. I explkt they will all be in Lord Derby War Hospital being well looked after in- stead of being out at the Front fighting. Well, do you know, Uncle Jack, I saw two soldiers coming home from the trenches yesterday, and although they were covered with mud and dirt they looked as happy as could "be When it is fine the soldiers in the hospital go out, and those that are not able to walk are allowed out in the fresh air. All round the hospital are gardens with fornis in, and they like to sit there and read. I think that if a soldier has been out at the Front fighting hard he is glad toget home and rest comfortable with his parents.', Niece Mabel Wright, 68, Winwick-street (Wycliffe School).—" I think there are many ways of bringing sunshine to the soldiers, such as taking them fruit and other things ,to cheer them up. Plenty of people go to visit the soldiers and drive them about in their own motor cars and traps, which the soldiers quite enjoy. Most of the soldiers are far away from their own homes, but they are made very com- fortable by the Red Cross nurses. It is a great pleasure for the soldiers to come to the hospitals out of the danger of these cruel Huns," ' Nephew Leo Fox, 70, Fox-street (Sacred Heart School).—" I think sunshine can be brought to the wounded soldiers in various ways, e.g., giving concerts to them, and when they hear some good old songs they will soon brighten up and oget their wounds. Another way is to send them sweet flowers and when they smell them they will be delighted to think that they are smelling the fragrance of the good old English country- side once more. The rich people could take the wounded warriors out for drives arta invite them to tea, and that would console the soldiers, and they would think that at least someone was thinking of them." tarnished gold. Shoulder draperies are to be the feature of this summer's frocks, and the newest are very frilly and ;ether floppy. Shawls, it is said, are to be revived in the near future, and shawl effects are doubtless being tentatively used in collars, tippets and fichus, in place of the starched muslin neck- wear that has had such a long reign. Some of the prettiest of the newest taffetas frocks are finished at the neck with shawl-like draperies that are apparently very easily arranged, for they look exceedingly simple. ' It takes an expert, however, to manipulate them in order to give a distinctive air to an otherwise plain dress. Simple summer gowns will depend chiefly for effect on these shoulder arrangements, which sometimes take the form of fly-away detachable capes. A New Umbrella.

In ever-increasing numbers women and girls are discarding the one-time indispensable um- brella, in favour of the waterproof outfit and sou'wester. Umbrella-makers have evidently taken note of the change going on, and have done their best to meet the demand for a less cumbersome umbrella than that of to-day, which is a vast improvement on the article In use even a decade ago. A novel and recent invention bears little resemblance to its pre- decessors. It is not more than two feet long, is very light and portable, for it is carried on the arm by means of a strong leather strap. It has apparently no handle, but the chances of losing it are reduced to a minimum, yet it affords the necessary protection from rain, is smart, and made in colours to match the owner's toilette, as well as in black. There are, however, very ornamental, long-handled umbrellas of the orthodox shape, also covered with silks in all the new shades, which in fine weather can be used as walking-sticks, since their bulk has been reduced to the, slimmest of slim proportions. As an elegant stick is quite in keeping with the ancient and pictur- esque modes of this summer, these umbrellas will doubtless be used in all states of the weather.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Diphtheria from Cats.

An outbreak of diphtheria in an orphanage has, says the National Medical Journal," been traced back to some cats in the building. On bacteriological examination it was found that the four cats from the boys' side of the orphan- age were suffering from the disease. Value of the Lemon.

Prudent housewives always keep a few lemons in the house. The following are some of the uses to which they can be put. Squeeze a lemon into a glass of water every morning and drink it with a little castor sugar; it will keep your stomach in the best of order. If you have a bad headache, cut the lemon into slices and rub these along yourlemples. The pain will not be long in disappearing or at ,least growing easier to bear. Salt and lemon juice will remove rust stains without injury to tne fabric. Dried lemon rind sprinkled over coals will destroy any disagreeable odour about the house. Mix equal parts of glycerine and, lemon juice to moisten the lips of a fevered, parched patient. A refreshing and appetising dish for an invalid is made by mixing the strained juice of a lemon with the. beaten white of an egg, to which add a tablespoonful of castor sugar.—" Food and Cookery."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
Value of Extract of Malt and Olive Oil.

Olive oil and malt is extremely useful in all forms of tubercular disease, whether pulmonary phthisis or bone disease. Next after fresh air and an outdoor life come the question of feed- ingin dealing with tubercular patients and the plain extract of malt, or better still, the com- bined malt and olive oil may be freely used spread on the bread as in the use of jam or eaten with porridge or puddings or taken in the form of a drink. Children suffering from rickets or any other form of malnutrition are always benefited by using malt and olive oil, and they seldom fail to take it with eager delight. Its daily use is one of the best means of preventing constipation or curing mild cases. Patients Isuffering from nervous debility, neurasthenia, hysteria, or other asthenic conditions, always benefit by the use of malt and olive oil, pro- vided they are not already overfed or too stout. —" Good Health."

(Arpley-street School).—" There are many ways in which children may strive to entertain and cheer the wounded Tommies.' Small presents such as fruit, flowers, cigarettes, walking sticks or books seem small to us, but it is surprising how pleased wounded soldiers are when they receive such gifts. Childish talk is the delight of many of the bedridden men in Winwick Hos- pital, because it is a reminder of happy times. Very often soldier have invitations to go to a friend's house just because the children take an interest in them. " In these ways many soldiers have been comforted and cheered dur- ing this terrible war."

Niece Elsie Johnson, 323, Lovely-lane (Wycliffe School).—" There are many ways in which we can bring streams of sunshine through or into the hearts of our heroes. If they have been badly wounded and are not expected to get bet- ter, but in a few days' time they begin to im- prove, then this is one way which brings a great stream of sunshine into their hearts, and gives them a better chance of bearing the long dais and hours with the cheerfulness of spirit. Another way which encourages soldiers is by telling them they can have a few days' leave to see the dear old home again, and its most dear and beloved inhabitants." _ _

Nephew Harry Heywood,"B, Matthews-street, age 9 (Parochial Day School).—" I am glad to become a member of the Children's Corner. I will try my best to win a prize and write my best also. I think the way to give the soldiers sunshine is to send them comforts like books to read and cigarettes to smoke, or take them ride to the country in a motor car." Niece Hilda Plant, 10, Goulden-street. (Arpley-street School).—" I am sure we could help to pass away some of the dull, monotonous hours that I am sure some of our wounded heroes are forced to live through, and the least we could do is to send a few comforts such as books, papers, chocolates and cigarettes to help to pass some \of their leisure time that hangs so heavily on their hands."

Nephew Ernest Sankey, 99, Kimberley-street (St. Barnabas' School).—" The wounded soldiers need cheering up, and I will tell you my ideas as to how it can be done. People who can afford it could get together a number of people and have a performance in aid of the wounded sol- diers. With the proceeds they could cover ex- penses and with the rest buy tobacco and cigar- ettes for the wounded heroes. This month and a part of last I was selling some draw tickets, half the proceeds for the Whitecross Military Hospital, which I suppose was for more material and clothes, which will make them well again. If any of us found any interesting books which we think the soldiers would like to read we could give them away, and those to whom we give them can pass them on to same more until they have all read them. They like playing dominoes, chess and draughts, so if any of us care to we could send them a game. Those who visit the hospital anti can play the piano should always give them ,a tune or sing them a song. The nurses when they are attending the men who cannot come out of bed could speak a kind word to them and tell them they will soon be better."

Niece Kathleen Smith, 43, Mill-lane (Evelyn.. street School).—" One of the many ways in which we can bring sunshine into the lives of wounded soldiers is by entertaining them in our homes, and sending them cigarettes, &c. We can also go visiting them and cheering them up by talk- ing to them. If we have any books that we have read we can take them to the hospitals for the soldiers to read." Niece Lily Williamson' 53, Priestley-street (Evelyn-street School).__" We can bring sun- shine into the wounded soldiers' hearts when we speak kind words to them and when we take clgare44€l4 .tobacco, anchnisgatines, &c., tothOm. - We ought to do all we can for them, for they

Nephew Lewis Geddes, 51, Laira-street (Ham- ilton-street School).—" I think music, motor drives, &c., are very good, but thre is nothing better after many months of absence than visits from their relatives and friends. Fancy! a hus- band to see his wife and dear little children, or a son his mother! What more sunshine could we give them than this?" Niece Edna Fellows, 44, Bewsey-road (Evelyn- street School).—" There are lots of wounded soldiers in Warrington at present, some of them perhaps thousands of miles away from their homes and friends. The least we can do is to take an interest in them. I myself have been to the Winwick Hospital and seen the soldiers. How eager they are for someone to take them out of the hospital grounds for an hour or so, and how disappointed they are when they are not the ones asked. There are many people who do not think about inviting the soldiers for a drive; or such like, who would, if it occurred to them to (do so. I remember once my father took two soldiers from the Winwick Hospital for a drive in the country, and then brought them to tea. One of the soldiers said that 11e was quite a stranger to England, and that he was many miles from his home. They were very thankful for the little pleasure ,that we had given them. if every able person in Warring- ton would help in such ways, it would very soon banish the sadness that seems to cloud so many soldiers' lives. That is the best idea, in my opinion. To take an interest in their welfare, and to let them know that they are not for- gotten by the people they have done their best to defend." c
