THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MAY 20, 1916.

TOWN COUNCIL

AND TRADES COUNCIL.

By a unanimous vote the Town Council declined, on Tuesday, to accept the nomina- tion of Messrs. CHARLES DUKES and A. E. SEABURY for seats on the Naval and Military War Pensions Committee. As will be seen from an official advertisements which appears on our front page, the MAYOR has' called e meeting of repiesentatives of all the Trade Unions in Warrington to meet at the Town Hall on Tuesday night for the purpose of nominating a representative of Labour to fill the vacancy which these rejections have left to be filled. What the Trade Unions in town should do is to call meetings of their members at once and eleet their representatives, with instructions how to vote, in order that their delegates may adequately represent the opinion of Labour, and not the opinion of the Trades and Labour Council, which, so far as war policy is concerned, is discredited through and through. It was because of the attitude the Executive of this body has adapted towards the war and recruiting that the Town Council declined to accept the nominations of these two ,leading officials. When the Wire Drawers seceded it was pointed out that when the' war was over trade questions and national interests would have to be reviewed, and that " Labour " ought so to manage affairs now that its representatives would be able to meet the capitali4 and gov- erning classes at least on an equality in the matter of patriotism. Is it too ranch to ask, ' even thus early, that Labour, which has done marvellously for the country; and will do more-marvellously yet, shall) by taking a for- ward glance, see that, step by step, those who speak in its name shall do so with authority? In the unofficial organ of the Public Schools Battalion, "The Gasper," the following pas sage occurs The country belongs to the men who have fought for it! Not to those who have stayed at home and reaped rich, profits from'tbe harvest of death; not to those whose "consci- entious objections" kept them in comfortable beds, paid for in the blood of others; not to those who clamoured for their "rights" while men were martyred for lack of munitions, but to us, the people, who have become—the Army. Theae-iwords have an ominous ring .about

===== 0002601_19160520_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR AMBULANCE FOR WARRINGTON.

At the last annual meeting of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee attention was drawn to the fact that when accidents occur in works there is sometimes much preventable suffering caused through the tardy arrival of the ambulance. As in the old days, when fires raged while horses were being found to draw the fire engine to the scene of the out- break, so suffering men and women now wait while horses are being found to take the am- bulance upon its errand of mercy. The work- ing men called upon the Town Council to make good this deficiency in the public equipment by providing themselves with a motor ambu- lance; but in these days of enforced economy public authorities are not able to spend an extra £4OO or £5OO at its mere suggestion. A convenient solution of the difficulty appears to have come to hand spontaneously. The local branch of the British Red Cross Society recently proposed to send a motor ambulance to the Front at an expenditure of £4OO. Half that sum has been subscribed, and it is now suggested that, badly as motor ambulances are needed at the Front, there is a still greater need for them locally where wounded soldiers are constantly arriving, and where the supply of conveyances is so small that the wasteful expedient of bringing cars and ambulances from Manchester has to be adopted. The subscribers have agreed to the diversion of the proposed ambulance from the Front to local purposes; and the assurance is given that at the end of the war it will be retained for the use of Warrington. .That ought surely to mean that the remaining £2OO will be found immediately.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING DEFERRED.

Expense And Shortage Of Teachers

Affects Oakwood Avenue School.

It having been decided by the School Manage- ment Committee to defer the opening of Oak- wood-avenue Council School, communications on the subject having been received from the War Office and the Board aif Education, Canon Morley Stevenson explained to the Education Committee on Monday, that the committee had cone to this decision with very great regret. It was due to the great expense of furniture and apparatus at the present time, and also to the difficulty of finding additional teachers. Mr. Edwin Plinston, chairman of the School Attendance Committee, added his regrets that such a course had had to be adopted, and he hoped that later on, when the usual communi- cations about the overcrowding of schools were received, the Board of Education would take the committee's action as sufficient reason.

The Education Committee agreed With the decision of the School Management Comritittee.

Scarcity of Leather in Germany. ,

An appeal, it is stated, has been placarded in the streets.of Strasburg by the relief committee, advising parents to teach children to go bare- footed during the summer. This exhortation, it is declared, has not only a hygienic purpose, but also a patriotic aim, as leather may be saved for a much more useful purpose."— "Leather Trades Review."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
No Leather Adulteration.

Boot manufacturers interested in the Russian order are expected to eover their requirements by May 16th, after which date tanners will make their returns of stocks of leather to the authorities, and they will decide what must be reserved and what may be sold for the home trade without restrictions. It is very interest- in. to note that all tanners in the country are producing leather of sufficiently good quality to be suitable for Government purposes, and it may be taken that there is practically, no adulteration going on, which is a great boon to the whole community.—" The Leather Warrington-verrenendent.

THE LATE MR. KENWORTHY.

taken part in the great 1868 election, and, being an expert raconteur, his recollections of that event were of the most interesting character. Elections nowadays are not always devoid of lively feeling, but on that occasion not a little. violence was shown, and Mr. Kelly orthy and several other leaders of the Liberal party once had a great difficulty in escaping from a Tory mob. As a recognition of his services, the then Chairman of the Liberal Association made him a presentation.

t he keenness of those days was maintained throughout his life. A hard worker and a generous subscriber to the Liberal cause in Warrington, he later became chairman of the Grappenhall District of the Knutsford Liberal Division, this being the Parliamentary area in which he resided.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTIST TO THE CORE.

Mr. Kenworthy was one of the worthiest of Baptists in the district. He came of a good old Baptist stock. His father was the Rev. Abraham Kenworthy, who devoted 37 years to ministering at the Hill Clifie Baptist Church. Soon after his father's death his brother, the Rev.' Samuel Kenworthy, succeeded him and remained there for several years. For. many years until leaving Warrington— Mr. Kenworthy was residing at Grappenhall when he resumed worshipping regularly at the Hill Cliffe Baptist Church—he attended the Wycliffe Congregational Church, where he was a highly-respected deacon, but his birth, train- ing and inclination all tended in the direction of the Baptist community. .Devoted in his worship, he also took a Most intelligent interest in the history of the denomination, and in 1882 published a short history of the Baptist Church at Hill Cliffe, following this a few years later with a well-bound volume elaborating in great detail the progress made right from the foun- dation of the church. The difficulty of the task which he undertOok'may be judged from the fact that the earliest evidence of the existence of the church . was found on a stone in the burial ground, bearing the date 1357, and he traced the history from that date until his work had the honour of being published by the Baptist Tract and Book Society. Until recent years Mr. Kenworthy always acted as a Sunday school teacher, first at Wycliffe and then Hill Cliffe. At the time of his death he was a trusted of the latter church.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
F R EEM AS O N AND LI FE-GOVERNOR OF !STOCKTON HE A_TH.

INFIRNIARY. Mr. Kenworthy was also a Freemason, being a member of the "Earl of Chester" Lodge, Lymm, and was also a life-governor of the Warrington Infirmary. He was married twice. His first wife, Miss Howson, daughter of Mr. Phillip Howson, of Grappeuhall, passing away in 1904. In 1908 Miss Clara Kenworthy, of Golear, near Hud- dersfield, joined him in holy matrimony, and she is now left to Mourn his death.

Mr. and Mrs. Kenworthy have often figured prominently at the opening ceremonies of sales of work in connection • with Nonconformist churches, and there is hardly any place of worship where, Mrs. Kenworthy has not ren- dered some beautiful contralto solos. The deceased gentleman is to be buried at Hill Cliffe this 'afternoon. The remains of his father and brother are interred there.

An Unwanted Tip.—The local Councils of Stockton Heath and Latchford Without have arranged to utilise the open space in Fairfield- road, Stockton Heath, as a tipping ground for the refuse of the various parishes. It need hardly be said that the proposal has created a strong feeling of resentment amongst the resi- dents and property owners in the vicinity, who are presenting petitions to the responsible parties protesting against such a course being adopted, on the grounds that the land is in the midst of residential houses, very near to the highvPays, and looked upon by the children of the surrounding district as their one and only playground.

MR. STEPHEN PLIMPTON.

Death From Heart Failure Following

Pneumonia.

We regret to record the death of Mr. Stephen Plumpton, managing director of Messrs. George Plumpton, Ltd., Albion Works, Warrington, the sad event taking place at his residence, The Cottage, Moore, on Tuesday night. An attack of influenza about five weeks ago developed into pneumonia. He was getting on nicely, but after three weeks he suffered a re- lapse, and passed away as stated from heart failure.

Mr: Plumpton was the son of the late Conn- Het was well known nldlorhiGgheolyrge Plumpton. respected, though, after business hours, he was not seen often in the town, finding cai in his home and garden needed recreation. He was also a breeder of dogs, and many of his terriers have gained successes at various shows. Before going to reside at Moore with his wife and three children, he attended Wycliffe Con- gregational Church, and has latterly worshipped at Daresbury Church.

WESLEYAN CHILDREN.

Record Collection At Sunday School Anniversary Services.

The Wesleyan Sunday school anniversary services were held on Sunday, the preacher, morning and evening, being the Rev. W. L. Doughty, of Huddersfield, whose eloquent dis- courses were much appreciated by the large congregations which assembled. In the afternoon a flower service was held, Mr. W. H. Sherburn presiding. The Rev. W. L. Doughty gave a most interesting address. A special feature of this service was the pleasing manner in which the children rendered their hymns, solos and recitations, great credit being due to those who had so efficiently trained them. During the day the choir rendered the an- thems, "Therefore with joy," "His Name is excellent," and "0 that men would praise the Lord," under the conductorship of Mr. F. E. Penketh, choirmaster, who had also trained the children' to sing the hymns used for the whole of the services, with Mrs. G. Gould as organist. A special appeal for ô. liberal collection was made by one of the scholars in a neat little speech, which, no doubt, assisted in raising a larger collection than has ever previously been obtained, viz., £l2 15s. The children's gifts of flowers were after- wards conveyed to the Raddon Court Hospital, where they were highly appreciated by the staff of the B.R.C.S. and the wounded soldiers.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 20, 1916.

GOSSIP.

I see that Warrington was represented at the Casement trial on Monday. The "Echo " stated that Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., sat on the Bench near to Sir John Dickinson, the magis- trate.

It has been very hot this week. You do not need telling that, I am mire. The coolest man in Warrington on Thursday afternoon was a Coolie, employed at a local ironworks, who was parading the town in a white silk jacket.

This hot weather is dumping men into hair- dressers' shops as well as into the country lanes. Besides making vegetables and flowers grow, the sun is increasing the stature of our beards con- spicuously.

Barbers confess that they do nearly twice as much shaving in summer as in winter.

"The Sketch" of last week contained an ex- cellent portrait of Lady Crosfield, showing her as a programme seller at the Tercentenary per- formance at Drury Lane. The King and Queen were present, and Lady Crosfield and others who sold programmes were attired in the cos- tumes of Old Drury orange girls of the Nell Gwynne period.

There was a very pleasant interlude in the pro- ceedings at the Distilet Tribunal on Wednesday afternoon, members taking respite from their labours in reviewing appeals by partaking of tea.

A member suggested that they should take tea at every future sitting, While the Chairman commented on the absence of a "distillery pro. duct." Nevertheless, a .cup of tea, with an abundance of cream, and daintily served by p. young lady clerk, made the members smack their lips.

For thc.• nonce the Rev. A. M. Mitchell and Mr. Mather were at one on the agreeableness (.f the rcfrohment.

Mr. Sam Brighouse, the coromyr-, is pi:l-long the frankest of our Public. officials.. Yesterday, at on inquest at, the Police Station he etracted an admission by a witness that his nickname. was ". Mick."

" Don't be ashamed .of it," he enjoined. "Most of us he—e i pieknara.?. People don't elways eall me Mr. Britzhoule. In Ormskirk I leiievc they Old Bam.' " But T 'should ”dvise all witness's to continu calling him " Sir!" OtherwiFe,,there might he another in- quest. . .

A single young man who appeared before the Tribunal this week complained that although his name and address were not given in the news- papers the reporter's description of his appeal was so accurate that he had been subjected to sneers from people. Certainly a tribute, to the care and accuracy always displayed by the local Press!

Mr. W. Stewart Royston, of Paddington, is much encouraged by the flourishing condition of his fund for walking-sticks for wounded soldiers. He is also naturally pleased with the appreciation which is beini, continually expressed by those who are benefiting by his anxiety to "lend a hand to comfort and help them," as an Edinburgh N.C.O. expresses it.

May I wish success for the matinee to be held at Lthe Palace and Hippodrome on Thursday, June Ist? It is in aid of a grand cause—the Blinded Soldiers and Sailors' Hostel—and a unique programme will be presented.

The Mayor and Working Men's Infirmary Committee are giving it their strong support. and a meeting of ladies at the Town Hall yesterday decided enthusiastically to sell tickets and programmes. Mrs. Henry Greenall has been appointed secretary of the Ladies' Com- mittee, sand application for tickets should be made to her at The Elms, Moore, or to -Mr: J. IL J. Hampson, at the Warrington Infirmary.

Perhaps those who cannot be present at the matinee will purchase tickets and send them to our hospitals for wounded soldiers. They will be seized with avidity.

The Office Boy, who often gets hold of some full-blooded literature, says that the following, sent by a Warrington competitor, has won a prize offered by a penny weekly:

A railway worker appeared at a local tribunal and claimed total exemption: The Mayor: "Is your work of such a highly important nature that you cannot be spared?" The Applicant: "Yes, sir." The Mayor: "Perhaps you will be so good as to explain the exact nature of the work you do?" •

Applicant: "Well, sir, you know that there man who taps them there wheels on all the trains at the terminus ? He's very deaf, and I

have to listen for him."

Need I add that this was not heard at War- rington?

MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

Bank Park And Hippodrome Concerts

In Aid Of Tfie Infirmary. The question of thd desirability of having a musical festival in connection with the War- rington Infirmary was settled in the affirmative by a meeting of the Working Men's Committee held in the Town Hall on Monday evening, Mr. George Walker presiding. Considerable discussion took place as to the form which the festival.should take.

It was thought by some that mixed choir& should again be requested, to present a pro- gramme, but, against this, it was pointed out that the cost of music would involve several pounds, whereas, especially in these days, they ought to economise. Also it was feared that as many of the choirs have been greatly depleted of their male members some choirs might not care to accept the invitation. Therefore, while being grateful to the singing community for their past services—(" They are, indeed, among the most charitable people we have," said one)— and contemplating the employment of their generous services in the future, they thought it well not to invite them this year. Instead, - Colonel Moggridge will be asked to allow the Band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment to perform in the Park in the after- noon, their programme to be augmented by one or two singers, Mr. Walker expressing the general willingness of Colonel Moggridge to do anything he could for Warrington institutions. With regard to the evening's programme, they thought of asking Mr. Jackson, the manager of the Hippodrome, again to allow them the use of his hall.

Just then Mr. Jackson himself arrived, and Mr. Walker said to him, "We were just talking about asking you whether we could have the Hippodrome for one Sunday night in August, in accordance with your usual . generosity," and Mr. Jackson promptly replied, amid applause, "There is no need for you to ask. The Hippo.= drome is there for you."

It was pointed out by the Secretary, Mr. J. H. J. Hampson, that last year £67 19s. 9d. was raised at the festival, the expenses being £l9.

A strong committee was -appointed -to take charge of the arrangements.

FORWARD, CLOCKS!

We Shall Rise An Hour Earlier And Not Know It.

To-morrow is the day when we begin to save an hour's daylight every day, and to-night's the night when we should put on the clock. Daylight saving simply means that before retiring to bed to-night we should move the fingers of our clocks- and watches forward an hour, and' if you are in the habit of getting up on Sunday mornings at 7 o'clock, or 12 o'clock, or, possibly, in the afternoon, you have just to stick to the hour by the clock as you have done hitherto. Of course, it involves the loss of an hour's sleep during the first night, but you will recover by Monday morning, when the alarm clock wakens you and indicates that you should get up to go to work. For you will have gone to bed the night before an hour earlier than usual.

The official regulation is that the clocks should be altered at 2 a.m. on Sunday, but this is just a little bit inconvenient for most of us, so we must do it to-night. Specifically at what time the Town Clock's fingers will be advanced depends upon the clock contractor himself, but we may be sure it will be all right before the general public is about to-morrow morning.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OVERSIGHT IN THE ACT.

A singular oversight has been made by the framers of the Act, for it is provided that the changed time shall operate under all statutes, - regulations and bylaws. The times for the closing of the public parks, pleasure grounds, bowling greens and public- baths are fixed by the respective bylaws and, consequently, the changed times will take effect here. The time for the closing of the parks in June and July is at present 9.30. The matter has not yet come before the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Com- mittee, but we are informed that. the Chairman has given instructions that no change shall at present take place, so that for June and July tht. hours of closing will be. 10.30 summer time. Alderman Bennett has sent. us an interesting letter rejoicing that the Daylight Saving Scheme is coming into force, and expressing his pride that the Warrington Chamber of Commerce has been a consistent advocate of it far several years.

EMPIRE DAY,

Scholars Celebrate it While

Teachers Fight For It.

There is every manifestation that Empire Day, on. Wednesday - next,/ will be celebrated with great fervour by our schools, though the programme does not include the picturesque ensemble of the whole of our school children iia Bank Park as occurred a few years ago. As in former years, however, teachers have been requested by the Education Committee to give appropriate lecturettes on the Day, and, no doubt, at -certain of the schools patriotic demonstrations will delight and cheer the chil- dren and onlookers.

Scores of teachers who have hitherto devoted May 24th to talking of the Empire are, now fighting to maintain its glory, and that fact alone must be a great inspiration to the scholars. The example of the ,absent teachers will be felt.

At the Education Committee mtret'ng on Mon. day Alderman Bennett and Mr. liolman King- don both appealed for the Day to be celebrated with even more vigour and enthusiasm than on any previous .occasion. Mr. Kingdon remarking that the lesson which .should be taught the scholars above everything else . was that our celebration was a tribute of admiration to the way in which the outlying portions of the Empire had supported us. (Hear, hear.) Our position to-day would not have been a nice one if it had not been for the very loyal and extra- ordinary way in which every part of the Empire had rallied round us, he concluded.

SALUTE THE FLAG.

(By the Rev. R. P. Downes. LL.D., in "Great Thoughts." Give Britain's banner to the breeze, Salute her Captains on the seas, Her heroes at the guns; Honour has called her to the fight, She faces in the cause of right, The, fury of the Huns.

True to their noble Fatherland, The brave lads of her Empire stand, Beside her in the field; India her whispered call obeys, And fronts with her the battle blaze, To strive and not to yield.

LICENSEE & SPORTSMAN.

Mr. T. Nixon Passes Away After Long Illness.

A well-known and highly-respected Warring- ton licensed victualler, Mr. Thomas Nixon, of the Black Bear Inn, Knutsford-road, passed away in theiearly hours of yesterday morning, after a long illness, borne with great fortitude.

The deceased gentleman, who was fifty-five years of age, had been in failing health for some months past, and since February had been confined to his bedroom.

Mr. Nixon was a man of grand physique. standing Oft. lin. in his stockings and weighing 17 stones. He was a keen and prominent sportsman, his principal• recreations being angling and shooting. In both these pastimes he was an expert, and, he was the proud possessor of many cups and medals, and some of his remarkable angling feats adorn th( rooms of his house, such an interested and keen fisherman was he.

A gentleman of genial and kindly disposition, he had many friends in the town and sur- rounding. district, and. lie will be missed at the many angling and shooting competitions. lie was a prominent official of the Warrington Anglers' /Association, treasurer of the Mersey Gun Club, president of the Latchford Garden Society, and treasurer of the Latchford Angling Society. He- was a member of the Wine and Beer Sellers' Association, also chairman of the Rational Friendly Society, his activities being of a varied and most useful character. The late Mr. Nixon was born in the houSe of which he was li6nsee, and it is interesting to relate that members of his family had re- sided there for over a hundred years.. In his younger days he worked Is a wiredrawer, being employed at Messrs. SileBEk's for fifteen years, efter which he became tenant of the Black Bear Inn.

In February last Mr. and Mrs. Nixon cele- brated their silver wedding anniversary. The deepest sympathy will go out to Mrs. Nixon and her daughter in their sad bereavement. _ _

The funeral will take place at St. Wilfr-id's Parish Church, Grappenhall, on Monday, the cortege leaving at 1 p.m.

WATCHING A FROG SWIM.

Excited Boy Falls Into Canal And Is

Drowned.

Playing in a field near the canal at Sankey Bridges, some first or second standard boys on Wednesday afternoon discovered a frog, and gleefully threw it into the water to watch it swim.

seven, of 70, One boy, George McGarity, aged Plumpton-street, got very excited, and kept jumping up and down on the towing path with the result that he slipped and fell into the canal and was drowned before assistance could be obtained.

The inquest was held at the Warrington Police Station yesterday morning, and a verdict of "Accidentally drowned" was returned. The reason for holding the inquiry at War- rington, although the body had been taken out of the water in the county, WAS that the Coroner, Mr. S. Brighouse, has recently made arrangements with the county and borough police whereby, after a body is recovered from the canal, it shall be conveyed to the home of the deceased, if it is in Warrington, and the inquest held at the Police Station, instead of being conveyed to the Chapel House, Sankey. Mr. Brighouse told the jury that as there was no ambulance in the Sankey district, a great deal of inconvenience tvas caused, and there was an unfortunate delay in the body being taken away from the public gaze. He was sure War- rington,' gentlemen would not mind being a jury under the circumstances, especially as the deaths in the canal only averaged -about--th•ree. a year. - ' ,

MR. JAMES KENWORTHY.

Sudden Death Of A Well-known Liberal.

ESTIMABLE GENTLEMAN.

In the sudden death of Mr. James Kenworthy at his residence, "Dunmore," Grappenhall, on Tuesday night, Warrington has last one of its worthies. Only a few hours befare Mr. and Mrs. Ken- worthy had returned from a holiday at Goicar, near Huddersfield, and when walking in their beautiful garden in the evening Mr. Kenworthy had remarked how well he felt. His death, soon after retiring to bed, therefore, came with the greatest surprise.' Excepting slight trouble from bronchitis, he had always enjoyed good health. •

The suddenness of Mr. Kenwortlly's death necessitated the holding of an inquest, which was conducted by Mr: Yates at the house on Thursday morning. The proceedings were of short duration, and a verdict of "Natural causes" was returned.

In the business, political and religious life of Warrington and district, Mr. Kenworthy was a well-known figure. Owner of a timber yard, abutting on Bewsey-street. he yet spared time to lend his influences in the direction of Liberalism. PENSIONS COMMITTEE.

Messrs. Dukes And Seabury Rejected By Town Council.

UNANIMOUS VOTE.

The Warrington Town Council, at a meeting called specially for the purpose on Tuesday, elected the local committee under the Naval and Military War Pensions Act. Principal interest was centred in the decision of the Council as to the nominees of the Labour party in town. In accordance with the official requirements of the scheme the Trades Council and the trade unions of the town had been asked to nominate five members or one-fifth of the proposed committee to represent Labour, and had submitted six names, as a result of a vote taken at a recent meeting of trade unions' secretaries. These six and the votes recorded for each were: Messrs. Dukes, 26; Plinston, 21; Baker, 19; Seabury, 16; Hodgson, 13; Rule, 11. It was assumed that the first five would be accepted. Of these, four were members—three of them prominent members—of the Trades and Labour 'Council who by their anti-recruiting policy and general hostility to the work of meeting the requirements of the war, have aroused much antipathy in the town, and particularly among the .working men, whom they have professed to represent. The constitution of the Pensions Committee was decided in General Purposes Committee, and when the members sat in open Council it was found that the nominations of Mr. Charles Dukes, secretary of the Trades council, and Mr. A. E. Seabury, his assistant, and the chair- man of the recent conference at Manchester of the British Socialist party when there was a split, had not been accepted. The committee is to be composed of 25 ladies and gentlemen, thirteen of whom are directly appointed by the Town Council, the others being nominated by various public bodies. As only four of the Labour representatives were accepted, there is a vacancy which the Labour party will be requested to fill. The General Purposes Committee was called for 4 o'clock, and it was after half-Past five when the reporters were admitted to the Coun- cil Chamber. The Mayor occupied the chair, and there were also Present the Deputy-Mayor • (Dr. Joseph), Aldermen Bennett; James Evans, John Evans, Smethurst and Tinnion; Council- lors Barton, Broadhurst, Chantler, Craik, Dale, Dickenson,, Fox, Gough, Henshall, Jenkins, Lashford, Lindley, Locker, Pemberton, Roberts, Wilkinson and Wilson, with the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. Hallaway). The Mayor said forthwith: I beg to .move the confirmation of the minutes of the General Pur- poses Committee, viz. t That the Naval and Military War Pensions Committee be composed of the following :

Councillor Broadhurst, Councillor Wilson, Alderman John Evans, Councillor Dickenson, Councillor Wilkinson, Mr. J. H. Smethurst, Mr. T. P. Bradshaw.

Mr. R. T. Fairclough, representing the Chamber of Commerce.

Lady Greenall, Mrs. George Dakin, Mrs. J. E. Seeker, representing the Soldiers' and Sailors' Families' Association.

Mr. H. Hope, Soldiers' and Sailors' Help Society. Mr. W. J. Rowland, Guild of Help.

Mrs. M. Jones; Mrs. Hy. Roberts, Ictiss White, Councillor Hy. Roberts, Alderman James Evans, Col, Moggridge. Mr. E. Plinston, Mr. F. A. Baker, Mr. F. Hodgson, Mr. R. Rule representing the Trades and Labour Council and the Trade Unions.

"These," concluded his Worship, "will be the members or this Pensions Committee, with the addition of another name, which we shall ask the Trades and Labour Council and the trade unions to nominate for our approval or other- wise. There has been some discussion about this matter, and it has been found that these are the four names which we, as a Council,. can approve out of the nominat:ons submitted to the Council." The Deputy-Mayor seconded, and the com- mittee as selected was unanimously agreed to. The proceedings occupied only two or three minutes.

MR. SEABURY'S COMMENT.

Mr. Seabury, who was discarded by the Town Council at Tuesday's meeting, informs us that he was nominated by a union—the Gas Workers and General Labourers' Union—which has more members than the whole of the other societies of the four who were adopted.

Motor and Bicycle Collide at Latchford With Harry Brear, of the Rock-road, sitting in the front carrier, another boy named William Stanley, of 28, Surrey-street, was cycling into Wash-lane on Thursday evening, when he collided with a motor car driven by Arthur Clarke, of 49, Napier-street. Brear was injured and taken to the Infirmary, where he passed comfortable night.

The causes of the 22 deaths in Warrington during the week ended May 13th were :—Whoop- ing cough, 2; phthisis, 3; cancer, 2; pneumonia, 3; premature birth, 1; dis. and accid, of par- turition, 1; heart diseases, 4; accidents, 2; all other causes, 4.

In the week ending May, 13th, there were in Warrington 33 births against 32 in the corre- sponding week last year. Of these 17 were males in each year; female babies numbered 16 last year and 15 this year. In the same week the deaths numbered 22; against 15 in the corre- sponding week last year,.

Mr. Jarman wrote stating that the place of meeting was a considerable distance from his home, and the meetings were usually held at an hour when it was impossible for him to attend.

. A new manager will be appointed at the next meeting.

Mr. T. Yuile's Funeral.

Impressive scenes on Saturday marked the funeral of the late Mr. Thomas Yuile, who was accidentally killed at the Vernon-street Tan- ning Company's Works on the Bth inst. The cortege was led to the Warrington Cemetery by ,the R.A.0.8. Band, of which deceased was a member, while a large number of members of the Order walked hi procession. The burial service was conducted by the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth, while a service was also conducted by Bro. J. Wolfenden. Mr. James Richardson and Mr. Richard Hewitt (represent- ing the directors and staff of the Vernon-street Tanning Company) and Bro. J. Holden (presi- dent of the Band) were amongst those pre- sent. Amongst many beautiful floral tributes there was one from "His Fellow Workmen," one from "His Friends," one from "His Neighbours," and one from the R.A.0.8.

Appointment for the Rev. W. Hendy

Cock.

The Rev. W. Hendy-Cock, who for the past five years has been vice-principal of the War- rington Training College, has been appointed assistant diocesan inspector for the North- Western Division of the Manchester Diocese, in succession to the late Canon Armitstead. This division extends from Blackburn northward. The reverend gentleman will also be at the disposal of the Bishop of Manchester for clerical work. Before coming to Warrington, the Rev. W. Hendy-Cock was senior resident master of the Exeter Residential Training College for eight years, during the last four of which he was also chaplain to the Exeter Workhouse. Locally he has assisted the Rev. G. C. Briggs with the Sun- day services at Christ Church, Latchford, and 'has conducted a very successful Men's Bible Class in that parish.

Monstre Garden Party in Bank Park. Favoured with fine weather, the garden party in Bank Park on Saturday next should prove a great and unique success, for hundreds of sol- diers' and sailors' wives and widows have been invited thither by the Mayor and Mayoress (Aid. and Mrs. Peter Peacock). It had been sug- gested by generous-hearted, but incompetent, statisticians, that the mothers might also have been included, and, no doubt, the host and hostess considered this, but were dissuaded by the enormous number for whom catering would be necessary. Had the invitation been of wider scope than it is, Bank Park would not have comfortably held the acceptors. Those who are to be present may be assured of a pleasant afternoon, for the Band of the South Lancashire. Regiment is to play selections, and Pierret en- tertainments are to be given by Mr. W. W. Whittle's troupe. Refreshments will be served at 4 . o'clock. Personal application for tickets should be made at the Parr Hall no later than Monday.

CONSCIENCES.

A Warrington Soldier's Searching Analysis,

THE NEED TO KILL GERMANS.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—l am come from the trenches, to which I went with my regiment 15 months ago. On behalf of my fellows out there," who went, each to do his share to defend Britain, and many of whom have given all, their lives, 1 would like to say a few words about the con- scientious objector. We feel that we are made to compare very badly with the consci- entious objector, who has an objection to taking human life. The assumption, then, is that we men, who have taken human life, take a delight in doing so, and that, therefore, the conscientious objector is on a higher plane, a superior being. We deny this heart and soul. During a fight we fight fiercely and well, but, a fight over, we avail ourselves of every oppor- tunity to help an opponent who is down. And We honour him, for is he not fighting for his Country as we are for ours, right or wrong? Objection to taking human life! Do not we and the enemy in front Object to it? How Otherwise account for the number of prisoners taken by both sides? Quite a number of my Own men, during an engagement, have lost their lives through attempting to remove prisoners to a place of safety, when it would have been quite simple to kill them. . . .

CONFLICTIN,3 TYPES OF CONSCIENCES.

We are puzzled, too, about the question of "conscience." Our " conscience " Sent us to Flanders, to suffer, fight and die to protect England. It, further, keeps us there, and will keep until the war is over, which We hope will be soon, with safety assured.

13ut what is this " conscience" which drives me, to Flanders, to fight to the death the Germans, yet prevents the conscientious objector from going? Since returning to duty from hospital and sick furlough I have had to do with one conscientious objector in detention. We chatted together on hooks, science, religion, &c. It was my business to see that his legitimate wants were attended to. He told me that he looked upon me as 'a gentleman "—that may mean anything; Ide not know. But one thing Ido know—l have myself killed Germans, men, and have been instrumental in the death of others. I have searched my conscience, and it does not condemn me. Why is this? My heredity and environment, so far as I could gather, have been, prior to the war, much the same as the conscientious objector's mentioned above. We belong to the same branch of the Christian Church, our reading has been much the same, and flo on. He calls his conscience the "Voice of God "—so do 1. mine. And so, still speaking in the name of the Britishers at the Front, I say that, taking us altogether, ve are at least as intelligent, as religious, as "good" as the conscientious objector; nay, until he dies for his conscience' sake, he is not as good as we. Which brings me to the crux of the question. Just now a lot of Precious time is being wasted over the ques- tion as to whether conscientious objectors should be put to death or not.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
110 ROOM FOR THE CONSCIENTIOUS

OBJECTOR.

The position presents no difficulties to the Then who are fighting. In England, at pres- ent, we are depending on the strength of our Arms, naval and military, for the essential things of life. These gone, we should have to depend upon what the enemy, victorious, pleased to grant us. Of the two alternatives, killing Germans or submitting to German rule, the majority of us prefers the former; the conscientious objector prefers the latter. Therefore there is no place in the world for him (for other nations in our ,place would do as we are doing), and no matter what a man be doing, either directly or indirectly, he is killing Germans. Then as regards his food, &c. All this only being obtainable through our naval and military strength, he should refuse to take and use; therefore he should die of starvation and hunger, voluntarily, if he wishes to be consistent. I fail to see anything dreadful in this. Thousands of men havo died in Flanders and other places " for the cause " ; the consci- entious objector should die for his " cause." But I certainly should not shoot him—we are still short of ammunition. \

Yours very sincerely, OLD SOLDIER.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
jOURNALISTIG "TOMMY"

IN INDIA. Mistakes Barber For Cobra In Early Morning.

A member of "The Examiner" staff has re- ceived the following entertaining letter, from a journalistic soldier friend On the cool verandah of a barrack block in India I sit and indite an epistle to a man of the world, a man of the past, the unreal past Wherein men shivered and swore at the cold. and longed for those few days of summer heat in which England is wont to bask. Here the sun shines day after day, week after week, with a steady, pitiless, eye-straining glare. :Mankind, ever seeking a pretext for indolence, decrees that the mid-day holirs must be devoted to rest by reason of the heat, and the result is that " Tommy's " life here is as close an approach to the proverbial bed of roses as is to be met with the wide world o'er. There is a Parade from 6.15 to 8.15 a.m., and from then onwards, generally speaking, we have the day to ourselves. Native servants and " bum-boys " clean our buttons, boots, messtins and every- thing else imaginable, as well as make our beds, so that we do little else but lie down, smoke ex- cellent cigars at two for an anna (Id.), eat fruit at dirt-cheap prices, and grow fat. With noth- ing much to do, we find the hot weather quite tolerable, as this is one of the coolest spots in India. We saw no hostile submarines on the run, though we were constantly ready to act in more ways than one until we reached Port Said. This IS a great country, and to see the native life makes one feel that it is worth while to do all in one's power to defend it. You can imagine how welcome home letters are out here, so please find time to write. If You would like to do me a great fav'our would You mail me your "English Review" after you have finished with it? We have some queer experiences. I was _awakened in the small hours of the morning by the gentlest of touches on the chin. Thoughts of cobras—the aftermath of a " snaky " con- versation of the evening—flashed through my mind, but on raising my eyelids with as much 'expedition as sleepiness permitted, I saw what was still sufficiently startling—a swarthy hratta countenance illumined by a lamp. -DaY thoughts switched round at once to my Portable property, as we had been warned against native thieves. Then the visitor spoke. Shave, Sahib?" he inquired. Before I was Sufficiently wideawake to say him yea or nay 1 Was lathered as I lay, and I sank into un- t'eusciousness once again, to the accompaniment Of the satisfying sound and touch of a keen zor manipulated by expert fingers. It was tee earliest shave I have ever had, and the most ;erafortable, too. Neither is it an expensive tliry, for it can be enjoyed daily for a month .fer the sum of two annas (2d.) The old country Dag something to learn yet. It has. Hairdressers, please note.

A CHARGE OF MURDER.

Formal Proceedings Pending The Holding 01 Inquest.

At a special magistrates' court, held at Peel on Tuesday morning, Sergt. John Williams, of the same company as the deceased, appeared on a charge that he "did feloniously, wilfully and of malice aforethought, kill and murder Colour-Sergeant Afalings, of the 2/ 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment

Mr. R. B. Moore, who conducted the prose- cution, explained that all he intended to do was to comply with the law by having the prisoner charged within 24 hours of his arrest, and to ask for a formal remand until after the inquest. Joseph Madigan, special assistant to the com- mandant at Knockaloe Alien Detention Camp, deposed: I was called to the sergeants' sleeping room of the 2/ 4th South Lancashire Regiment, in No. 2 Camp, yesterday afternoon, about 3.30. I saw the body of William Henry Malings. He appeared to have been recently killed. Owing to a number of circumstances the prisoner, John Williams, was apprehended in connection with the death.

Prisoner did not wish to question witness. Mr. Mciore said the prisoner was not repre- sented by counsel. Arrangements would be made to have him legally represented. In the meantime he would advise him to say nothing at all at the proceedings that day. P.C. W. H. Clague deposed: I attended at Knockaloe Camp yesterday in company with Inspector Shimmin, and received into custody Sergeant John Williams. Inspector Shimmin read over the charge to him and cautioned him in my presence. Prisoner made no reply to the charge. He was then asked if he had any state- ment, to make, and he said, "No, not at present." Prisoner was remanded till Monday.

THE INQUEST.

Coroner States The Circumstances

Anti Adjourns.

The inquest on the body of the deceased was opened on Tuesday afternoon before Coroner J. S. Gell, in the Patrick Schoolroom. The Coroner explained that Colour-Sergeant Malings, one of the guards engaged in Knock- aloe Alien Camp, was in his room with another man—Sergeant John Williams. A shot from a carbine was heard, and on the room being entered Malings was found on his. back dead. There was little doubt but that he died from being shot—he was not going to venture to say by himself or the other man. He only proposed to take evidence as to the identity of the dead man, and was going to adjourn the inquiry with a view fo further information being received in the matter. He wished to tell Williams, who was the only man in the room at the time, that he wanted him to be legally represented. As, Williams was the only man in the room, he (the Cordner) would put questions to him, which he might or might not answer, and it would be a

NEWS OF THE TRAGEDY.

Pathetic Station Scene At Warrington.

Mrs. Malings and her daughter Vera went to the Isle of Man on Tuesday, in response to a telegrara received from Captain Stokes, who stated, "Colour-Sergeant Malings has been seriously injured. Come at once." This was received on Monday afternoon, and Mrs. Malings immediately telegraphed for further par- ticulars, and on receiving none she decided to make the journey to Peel by Tuesday 'morning's boat, still think- ing that her husband was alive, and knowing nothing of the circumstances under which his injuries had been sustained. It was while Mrs. Malings and her daughter were standing on the Central Station waiting for their train to arrive that Horace Malings, a bright fourteen-year-old lad employed in the office of Messrs. Pearson and Itnowles Coal and Iron Co., ran'up to them and displayed a news- paper cutting alleging that her husband and their father had been shot. It was a distressing, incident, the family bursting into tears, with Mrs. Malings almost fainting. Fortunately, a lady friend was by her side. It was question- able whether Mrs. Malings and her daughter would be well enough to travel—in fact, friends requested them to delay now that they knew the Colour-Sergeant was beyond human aid—but they decided upon making the journey.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVED IN THE BOER WAR.

Malings was well-known to the inhabitants of Warrington, his business as a hairdresser, his membership of the "Duke of Devonshire" Lodge of the R.A.0.8., and his position as master of the Junior Conservative Bugle Band, having afforded him some prominence in the public eye. Having served with the Cheshire Yeomanry throughout the Boer War—he was in the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, but was transferred —it was only to be expected that soon after the present war began he should volunteer his ser- vices. His battle experiences gained in South Africa were particularly valuable as an in- structor in Kitchener's Army, and he relished the work, but ill-health generally and rheuma- tism particularly, compelled him to take his die- 'charge in the following, December. A consider- able rest and a feeling that he would have to remain a civilian led him to accept employment at the St. Helens Cable Works. He stayed there until July, though continually expressing his desire to be again in the Army, and then he managed to get into the National Reserve, and was drafted to an Isle of Man Deteraion Camp after a few weeks' training. He enjoyed three or four "leaves" at home since going there, the last one being in January. Moreover, he intimated last week that he might possibly he returning this week-end for an extended holiday, as ill-health had prevented him discharging his duties effectively for two or three months.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
TYPICAL SOLDIER'S HOME.

Malings's home in Liverpool-road is typical of a keen and intelligent soldier's residence. Hanging on the walls as exemplars of patriotic duty and reminders of past years, are large portraits of Lord Kitchener, Lord Roberts and General Baden-Powell; a black-and-white of Nelson's death at Trafalgar; and the stripes and crown worn by his late father, who worked with the Press Gang in the early part of the nineteenth century. Another picture is the R.W.F. memorial to those who fell in the Boer War, inscriptions of the engagements fought by them being printed thereon. On the mantel- shelf in the front room stands a cabinet por- trait of Captain Stokes, who sent the fateful telegram to Mrs. Malings regarding her hus- band. pn one side of the hall there are grouped ancient bayonets, arranged as spokes in a wheel, with a horseshoe at the axle. Be- low is a sabre. On the other side two rifles, horiz9ntally fixed, provoke recollections of the Napoleonic wars, and close by a British arrow- gun stands out from remoter history: Malings was also associated with the Legion of Frontiersmen as Sergeant-Trumpeter. His brother Harry was wounded in the head in the Boer War and subsequently died in Denbigh Hospital.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====

Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd.; Horace (14). in the office of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd.; and Edwin (12), who *attends Evelyn-street Council School.

Mr. A. H. Pollen Postpones Warrington Visit For A Week.

COLONEL FAIRCLOUGH HOME.

His Opinion Of Walking Day

And Consciences.

BATTALION COMFORTABLE

Colonel Brereton Fairclough, commanding 'officer of our local Territorials, has been home this week on a short leave of absence. The gal- lant officer, who a few weeks ago was in Eng- land to receive at the hands of His Majesty the honour of C.M.G. (Companion of the Most Distinguished Order of St. Michael and St. George) was unable then to come North, but returned forthwith to duty. He is in the hest of health and spirits, but when seen by our representative on Tuesday morning was unable to say anything about the military situation or about the location, strength, or duties of his battalion. He was, however, anxious to speak very well of every- body under his command and to say how com- pletely and successfully all are pulling together. "The general health of the men," their friends will be glad to know, said the Colonel, "is exceedingly good—much better than the average of battalions at the Front. They are all going on very comfortably indeed."

"What do the Vifarrington men think of the resumption of Walking Day?" our representa- tive asked. "The news had not reached the Front when I left," he replied, "but / was delighted when I saw the papers on reaching home, and I am quite sure all Warrington men will be pleased to know the youngsters are not to be denied their annual treat this year."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSCIENCE AND QUALMS OF CONSCIENCE.

Colonel Fairclough seemed to be more inter- ested in consciences than war conditions out in France or Walking Days at home. "The men out there," he volunteered, "have decided opinions on this question. If those who profess to have consciences only knew what was being said of them by those on active service they would have some qualms of conscience, I can tell you."

===== 0002601_19160520_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAMMAR SCHOOL CADETS INSPECTED

At his own request, Colonel Fairclough on Wednesday morning inspected the Grammar School Cadet Corps in the grounds of the school. He did so in the capacity of command- ing officer, for the Cadet Corps is attached to the local 'Territorials. There was a full muster on parade, and after a march past and the salute the Cadets were put through various evolutions and drills, which . they did with a smartness speaking well for them and their officers.

The Colonel, in a short speech to the Cadets, expressed himself as well satisfied with them. They had done all their movements u, ith much smartness, and he would ask them to pay much attention: even to the smallest detail, and took several illustrations from the present war. Captain Pearson was in charge of the Cadets. Others present at the inspection were the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis), Aldermen ;T. Smethurst and A. Bennett, and Councillor IT. Roberts.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
LT.-COL. CROSFIELD, D.S.O.

Back In England, Well And In The Best Of Spirits.

Lieutenant-Colonel Crosfield arrived in Eng- land from France this week and was admitted to hospital, 9, Grosvenor Gardens, London. Although he was in danger for a time he has since completely conquered the septic poisoning and is making excellent progress. In fact, it is expected that he will be out of the nurses' hands in less than a fortnight, when he will take up his quarters with Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield, at West Hill, Highgate. As the amputation was well below the knee it is now confidently expected that Colonel Crosfield will eventually suffer comparatively little incon- venience.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS TITLE.

We have hitherto been in doubt as to the correct title of the gallant officer, owing to the uncertainties of the conditions connected with his recent commands, but Major Crosfield has now been gazetted Lieutenant-Colonel, which settles the point conclusively and satisfactorily.

LIEUT.-COL. LINDSAY MENTIONED IN DISPATCHES.

Lieutenant-Colonel C. Hutchinson Lindsay, of the R.A.M.C., and well known in Warrington and district as a medical practitioner at Widnes and a member of the ,Mersey Golf Club, Fiddlers' Ferry, has had the honour of men- tion in General Sir lan. Hamilton's supplemen- tary dispatch. Colonel Lindsay went through the whole of the Gallipoli campaign and has been mentioned in dispatches no fewer than three times, as well as having had the distinction of being awarded the "Most Distinguished Order of St. Michael and St. George." The recommendation from the General in Command' of the 29th Division for the C.M.G. reads:—" For marked ability in command of the 87th Field Ambulance, and for conspicuous courage and coolness under fire on many occasions. No task has been too danger- ous for him to undertake, and his , organising powers have ensured successful completion of what was undertaken. Gallipoli Peninsula, June 13th, 1915."

NAVAL RESERVE SIGNALLERS WANTED.

A Rare Opporlunity To Do Good, Useful Work.

At the moment the Navy presents a unique opportunity to ambitious young men of gooi education and physique to take a hand in strangling our enemies. There is a need for material .to be trained as signallers for the Royal Naval Volunteer Reserve, and as the need will not last long, it would be well for those who have a leaning for really first-class work at sea at once to consult our Medical Officer of Health, Dr. G. W. N. Joseph, the Health Offices, San- key-street, who has the matter in hand. Appli, cants were expected to be between 18 and 28 years of age, but this week Dr. Joseph has been informed that very energetic men up to the age of 33 will be accepted temporarily. Men can be transferred from the Army groups to this signalling branch of the Navy, the status of which is somewhat similar to that of the Public Schools Battalion in the Army. The work is very attractive and the three or four months' training takes place at the Crystal Palace, London, in ideal conditions. The men join for the duration of the war, and will have the advantage of being the first to be discharged on the cessation of hostilities. There is no immediate service, but as vacancies occur in the depots the men will be called ult.

MR. CHARLES DUKES.

Unsuccessful Anpeal To The

Manchester Tribunal.

STRONG MILITARY OBJECTION.

The application of the Gas Workers and General Labourers' Union for the exemption of Mr. Chas. Dukes on the ground of indispensability having been refused by the Manchester Tribunal, he presented himself to the Appeal Court at Manchester on Tuesday afternoon.

Mr. Dukes' case was the thirtieth on the list, and he had the tedium of sitting in the Court well over two hours while these were being dealt with. He was wearing his reddish-brown Trades Council suit, Accompanying him was Mr. Fleming Eccles; district secretary of the Gas Workers and General Labourers' Union, who was present to testify to the applicant's indis- pensability to the Society of which hells a dis- trict organiser. Over the Appeal Tribunal, Mr. Charles Ains- worth, 'of Bolton, presided. Business here is transacted expeditiously. Applicants are in the chair and out again "in no time." They are told to keep to the point, are then pertinently questioned, and soon know their fate. Mr. Dukes held the fort for six minutes—almost silently, for Mr. Eccles, being classified as the employer, did the talking. 'Mr. Dukes is responsible for all questions arising among 15,000, workmen," began Mr. Eccles, as soon as, the Chairman looked up from the appeal form. "He is the person 'who has sole control of a large area, and at present there are forty-six separate and distinct disputes. be- tween workmen and their employers." _

The Chairman: I think we lia;,re had a similar case from the same people with another repra- sentative.

Mr. Dukes: That was the case of a clerk

The Military Representative (to Mr. Eccles): How long has he been in this present employ- ment ?

Mr. Eccles: Seven months. When this case was before the local (Manchester) Tribunal the Chairman agreed that Mr. Dukes was engaged on work of national importance. That expres- sion is fortified by the newspaper report of the case.

Mr. Eccles then handed to the Chairman "The Examiner" report of the previous pro- ceedings, the particular irssage referred to having been underlined.

Thereupon, the military representative, quick in counter-attack, briskly rifled his papers; and, producing another copy of "The Examiner," said, "And I also put in an underlined passage where the Chairman said that Mr. Dukes speaks with no authority."

The Chairman: We will not dispute about these things.

DIFFICULT TO GET SUBSTITUTE.

Mr. Eccles: I am not concerned about the man (Mr. Dukes). What is troubling me is his work. I am speaking here on behalf of the Union. He is engaged on work of national importance, and has been acting in this present capacity for seven months. Before then he held an onerous and responsible position in connection with the Union in Warrington for six or seven years. We regard his work as being very important, and do not consider it is wise, even if *e could, to change the position of a true and tried officer. A Member of the Tribunal: Is he married or single?

Eccles: Single. Almost every day we are submitting cases of dispute to the Chief Indus- trial Commissioners and the Board of Trade.

The Chairman: How many men from your Union have enlisted? Mr. Eccles: Over 6,000. How many have gone from the office ?—Two. How many organisers of the some standing as Mr. Dukes P—None, but they have taken the only clerk we have left.

How many organisers have youP—Three,

Here the military representative indicated that Mr. ,Dukes .is also secretary of the War- rington Trades and Labour Council. Continuing, Mr. Eccles referred to the sue,,,,,,res- tion of the Chairman of the Court below that the Union could get another man to take Mr. Dukes' place. Their reply to that was that they had difficulty to get men to keep the local branches going. They had in their district

ninety branches, and three-fourths of the

officers had been called up now. The Chairman: Have you put appeals in? Mr. Eccles was understood to reply thtit they were going to do so. He added that they had had to close some of their branches because they could not get secretaries. The Chairman: Have you tried women? Mr. Eccles: We cannot do that. The -Union consists of so many different grades of men that a woman could not do the work. It is not the kind of work for a woman.

The Chairman: Is not a woman very symna- theticP—lt is not sympathy; rt is sometimes the other side. (Laughter.)

The Chairman: All right. I only wanted to know.

INDISPENSABLE LABOUR OFFICIALS.

Mr. Eccles added that he had just received a telegram from Mr. J. R. Clynes, M.P., the general secretary of the Union, telling him that the Labour representatives in Parliament and the leaders in the Trades Congress had arranged to give the Government a list of their indis- pensable officials; and he (Mr. Clynes) therefore asked for a postponement of Mr. Dukes' case. The telegram was handed to the Chairman, who showed it to his colleagues, making no pub- lic comment.

The case for the applicant being concluded, the military representative said: We object to this case very particularly. This is an applica- tion from the Secretary of the Gas Workers ani General Labourers' Union, whereas Mr. Dukes is really the secretary of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council. Mr. Eccles: He is a full time servant of our 'Union. The position of secretary of a Trades Council is never taken up by a full time man. The Military epresentative :Is Mr. Dukes Isecretary of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, or is he not?

Mr. Dukes: It is a spare-time occupation. The Tribunal consulted among themselves for a moment and then the Chairman remarked: "We are of opinion that the application should be dismissed," simultaneously stamping to that effect Mr. Dukes' appeal form. Mr. Eccles: Then may we appeal to the Central Authority? The Chairman looked at his colleagues, who shook their heads, and, vocalising their ideas. he said, "We have considered the appeal in the question of fact, purely and simply, so there is no need to appeal." Mr. Eccles: So we cannot have another appeal?

. _ The Chairman: No. Mr. Dukes was then directed to the clerk's table, where a young lady handed him in black and white the decision of the Tribunal, smiling sweetly as she extended the fluttering paper. It was a bright touch to a sad ending. But she had the same sympathetic play of features for every. body—successful or unsuccessful.

PRIVATE C. JOHNSON.

Missing Sixteen Months ; Now Reported Killed.

After being wbunded and missing since Dec- ember 20th, 1914, following an engagement at Givenchy, Private C. Johnson, son of Mr. Thos. Johnson, 109, Sharp-street, is now officially re- ported killed.

- Private Johnson,, who belonged to the Man- chester Regiment, was 33 years of age. He saw ten years' service. in India and was drafted from there to France in October, 1914. It is twelve years ago since he last visited home, when he came from Ireland prior to sailing for India. Before enlisting he was employed by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., first in the plate mill and later in the boiler yard. He was educated at Hamilton-street School.

Sergeant A. Edward Downing, son of Mr. Alfred A. Downing, 2, Winmarleigh-street, was wounded by a bullet in the right forearm on May 6th, and is now in a Manchester military hospital, making satisfactory progress. Sergeant Downing was a wiredrawer at Whitecross when the war broke out; he was one of the first to enlist and has been on active service for over a year. He was secretary of St. Paul's Cricket Club.

Son Of Ex-Alanager Of Richmond's

‘Vorks Missing.

In the list of officers reported missing has appeared the name of Second-Lieutenant C. L. Davy, of the Royal Warwickshire Regiment. He is the only son of Mr. J. F. Davy, Sherwood, Wylde Green, Birmingham, formerly works manager for Messrs. Richmond's, Warrington. Educated at Solihull Grammar School, he was company-sergeant-major of the School Officers' Training Corps when the war broke out. He left school at.once, and obtained a commission in the Royal Warwickshire Regiment. He has been on active service since October last. He is only 19 years of age.

-

STAGE AND KINEMA.

Royal Court.

Of the six or seven companies which Miss Henrietta Schrier and Mr. Lodge-Percy have on the road, "-The Blind Girl's Rosary" is one.

Curiously enough, the dude of the show also happens to be the business manager, and a rare, good and shrewd fellow he is. He has attested in the Royal Marine Artillery. lie knows more than a little about ships. For several winters he used to haunt Cornwall, watch boats, large and small, break on the rocks, and then wire stories to the "Daily Mirror." One season he saw 153 in distress and, not infrequently, .aug- mented lifeboat crews.

Mr. Jordan has had a spell of managing. Last year for five or six months hr governed the Theatre Royal, Birkenhead, and then returned to the road. Residence in one town for so long did not suit him. Actors, as well as gypsies, are nomadic by nature. That period of six months on Merseyside is, by a long time, the most ex- tended he has been stationed in one town for ten years.

When he came away the patrons gave him a purse of gold. Ile howed me the purse....

Campbell Goldsmid, .who takes the part of the engineer, Strong, has spent almost as many years out of the country as in it. Ite has been to the Royal Court many times, and is a worthy favourite.

From 1900 to 1902 Mr. Goldsmid toured the Antipodes with WilSosr Barrett, and alighted in South Africa in 1901. They covered the distance from Durban to Johannesburg in an -armoured train; theirs being the only theatrical company allowed to travel. - They were held up on the Tugela for 121- hours, "• while a little scrap was going on a few miles away. We could' hear the firing, and while we waited Vie visited young Roberts's grave situated close by us."

Miss Lilian MaitlaniT, the leading lady, has been on the .stage since eight years old. Her debut was as Little Willie in "East Lynne." She has developed since then, and last year was leading lady on the guliver Circuit—one of the biggest circuits in London..

Next week's attraction will be the well-known play, "Bella Donna," .adapted from the novel of that title by Robert •Ifichens. Mr. James Bernard Fagan's work in adapting the play has been excellently carried out, and the atmos- phere created is perfect; in fact, I prefer it to the book, And that is wonderful enough. There is splendid concentration in the play.

One feels that one has. indeed had a real glimpse of Egypt, with all its mysterious charm and hidden possibilities. And throUgh all the beautiful scenes is woven in perfect pattern the story of love and hate, of calculating crime and keen-edged persistency in its undoing, that drew all London to the St. James' Theatre for nearly a year. The lighting effects are remark- able.

The company, which is being presented by Messrs. Dudley Stuart and Gerald Alexander, is a very strong one, and includes Miss Georgina Wynter as Mrs. Chepstow (Bella Donna). •

lippodrome.

Cullan. and Carthy, the songsters and patterers, we all know, but I don't 'think the Wee Fellow they have brought with them has been here before.

The adults picked up this droll adolescent five years ago when he was singing comic songs in the seventh standard.

Cullen and Carthy's first introduction to War- rington was at the Theatre Royal, Scotland. • road.

These two have been here in pantomime several times. "We are favourites in Warring- ton," they said to me. "Of course, you are staying here?" I presumed. "Oh, no, we catch the 9.50 to Manchester every night." Which reminded me that once a Philadelphian said the finest thing about Boston was the 11.40 express out of it.

A. D. Robbins is a wonderful cyclist., He had several years in Canada with bucking horses, and now rides a bucking bicycle. Ms offer of £5 to anyone in the audience who can ride it round the stage five times has been accepted, but not won. During the six or seven years he has had no one has been successful.

Robbins bas toured moSe lA' the world, and Is.

usually where he has appeared his name has been in the biggest letters on the bill. While performing he has broken his shoulder no fewer than four times, and that is why he blended his "sheer trick riding' with comedy. The strain of -just doing tricks' is too'great for-him.

Paul Stephens, the one-legged equilibrist, is not a victim of the war, but of a street accident long years ago. An American, he arrived in England in January, and has been performing his wonderful way every week since.

The funniest comment he ever heard made about his turn was, "Well, if a one-legged man can do it there ain't much in it!" But there is.

I have just seen the\ latest annual report of the Bradford and District Army Veterans' Association, which was started by Mr. Jackson and a police inspector. There it is stated: "The veterans had the honour of attending the Palace Theatre matinee on Thursday, Septem- ber 2nd, when an address (in black and wllite), the work of one of the veterans, was presented to the former manager. Mr. W. A. Jackson, in recognition of his invaluable assistance during the formation of the Association."

Frequenters of the circle bar will recall this beautiful address, which finds an honoured place on the walls. It is the work of a fine soldier, well over 80 years of age.

In Sankey-street the other day I walked into,. Fred Marshall, the ex-assistant manager of the Palace and Hippodrome, who went to make a fortune in the U.S.A. about half a dozen years ago. It was a hearty handshake we gave each other.

He was looking splendid in the uniform of the Canadian Grenadier Guards, his red-hued skin blazing health. And, shooer, he talks just like . . an Amuriean!

Several vaudeville favourites will make their appearance next week. The Ten Loonies, bur- lesque musicians, *ill top the bill, and their turn is undoubtedly one of the most amusing musical creations on the stage at present. It is interesting to record that seven late members of the company are now serving. Ben Albert, a comedian with a "taking style," foots the bill, and another prominent artiste is Oswald Ber- nard, the well-known illusionist.

EXTRA OUT-RELIEF.

Differepce Of Opinion Between The Warrington 8; Runcorn Guardians.

At Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians a letter was read from Mr. A. Bottomley, clerk to the -Warrington Board of Guardians, asking the Runcorn Board to. give a reason for the refusal to grant extra relief recommended to a woman residing in Warring- ton, and for whom the Runcorn Board accepted

The Clerk (Mr. Hough) said he understood the Warrington Guardians had decided to lay the facts before the Local Government Board, but no notice of this in- tention had been given the Runcorn Guardians. He suggested thait in view of what had taken place and until some communication was received from the Local Government Board, no particulars should be given the Warrington Guardians.

After Mr. Pilling had expressed the opinion that there was no reason for the granting of extra relief, the Chairman (Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun.) thought no good would be served by dis- cussing the matter until they heard from the Local Government Board.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT WANTED.

Runcorn R.D.C. on Employment Of Conscientious Objectors.

At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, held at Dutton on Tuesday, the Clerk read. a letter from the Rural District Council Association , intimating that the Government had appointed a committee to which a Tribnnal might refer for advice as to what service of national importance an applicant for exemption on the grounds of conscientious objection should. undertake, and for such other important work as the Tribunal might require. The committee

had prepared a list of occupations which might be regarded as constituting work of national importance, among which had been included: Sanitary services of local authorities, fire brigade, civil hospitals and asylums. The com- mittee had asked the •Association to obtain re- plies to the following questions for its informa- tion in order that it might be able to give advice which would be of value to the Tribunals:— Would theY Council te willing to engage men having a conscientious objection to ,military service, provided they were suitable ? ; •witat number of vacancies was there in the staff 1. f each department of the Council which they de- sired to fill?

Mr. C. E. Linaker, sen., declared that their objection to taking conscientious objectors was unabated. "We want none of them," added the speaker. Other members declared that they were unanimous on the point. The Clerk: Then I will reply stating that the Council are not willing to fill any vacancies in this way.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
PATRIOTIC OPFER.

Runcorn Guardians Thanked

By Military Officer.

The proposal to utilise a part of the Runcorn Workhouse at Dutton as a military hospital is, in the words of Mr. Pilling, "off," and the announcement was made at a 'meeting of the Guardians held on Tuesday. The Clerk (Mr. W. E. Hough) -read a letter from Colonel W. Coates, A.D.M.S., Western Command, in which he stated that he had re- ported to the Deputy Director of Medical Ser- vice the result of his inspection of the Work- house premises. He had informed him that the accommodation offered would provide for some twenty-five patients, and with a little structural alteration, and the addition of baths, &c., they would be made very comfortable. He agreed with him (Colonel Coates) however, that it would not be at all likely that the War Office would take over this accommodation on the lines laid down by the War Office and the Local Government Board.

'lle only way in which use could j;te made of the accommodation offered would be on the lines of an auxiliary hospital, such as were provided by the Red Cross Society, St. John Ambulance Association, and some times by private com- mittees. On these lines a grant of 2s. per day per patient was made. Colonel Coates concluded "If, therefore, at some future date we are very much pressed for beds I shall venture to enter into correspond- ence with you again. In the meantime I am directed to thank the Guardians for their very patriotic offer of .help in this great national crisis." Mr. Pilling: It is off, then.

Mr. C. Lin-aker, jun. said Colonel Coates was much impressed with the accommodation, but it was not big enough to warrant it being made into a military hospital. He seemed to come with the intention of taking over the whole of the Workhouse. The matter would remain in abeyance for a time at, least. The subject then dropped.
