LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
By CRITIC.

The children attending Lymm School are apparently taught to take an interest in local affairs and those who control them, for in the girls' department there was recently put to the scholars the question, "Who is the most popular gentleman in Lymm ?"

The girls, naturally, had a good selection of names, and, of the severarthousand inhabitants, the Rector • (the Rev. G. B. Thurston) and the Clerk to the Urban District Council (Mr. W. MuUnit) were first and second respectively.

Some very estimable gentlemen were "also ran.s." At what stage in the list the village policeman figured, I do not know.

Lymm has laid to rest this week one of its most popular residents, Mr. A. B. Boumphrey, of Baycliffe.

Mr. Boumpbrey always tOok a; great interest in the village, and his generous response to all chatity appeals will be greatly missed.

Major Alexandra, D. 5.0., is beginning to look quite his "old self" again.

This week two of our local soldiers, who were home from the Front, have ,had the honour of an introduction to the Mayor of Warrington.

It ig to be hoped that they do not develop swelled heads.

Everything weiit well at Wormald Green on Wednesday.

The open-air concert proved a great attrac- tion, and the artistes were evidently at their best.

Mr. Harry Gibbon's " Tut-tut " proved to be quite a rough diamond.

Another star Aim was Mr. Groun.ds's imper- sonations. He took the old dame off quite to perfection.

Is it right for a wounded soldier, wearing a i)anatna hat, to raise, it when approaching a .ady

Or ought he to salute in the usual way?

I saw a lady in charge of four " Tommies " busily carting chairs to Wormald Green on Wednesday morning.

One of them afterwards told me that she was he finest "N.C.0." he had ever worked for.

An Apology.—Owing to being Rxtremely busy we have been compelled to keep Private E. Shaw's monthly diary over until next week.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM HIS COMRADES.

Wedding Gilt From The Trenches.

It will be remembered that during Private 3. Read's furlough from the, Front some time ago, he took unto himself a wife. The news created considerable interest among his Lymm comrades in the trenches, who were quite taken by surprise. They decided that at the first opportunity they would make him a present. This opportunity very soon came, for when Sergeant-Major H. Clare left for home, he was entrusted. with the honour of putchasing a beautiful hall clock and prAenting it to their comrade's bride, with the compliments and best wishes for a life-long happiness, from the Lymm boys in the trenches. These orders were faith-

fully carried- out. A clock was purchased from a local jeweller, and the presentation took place last week-end by Sergeant-Major Clare and Private Richard Shaw, both comrades of the happy bridegroom.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW FIELDS SOLDIER. KILLED IN ACTION.

One Of A Patriotic Family.

News was received yesterday to the effect that Private John Statham, a*Newlields soldier, had made the supreme sacrifice. The news was con- veyed through, an official letter sent by the keeper of records, and received by his wife, which read as follows:

'lit is my painful duty to inform you that a report has been received from the War Office notifying the death of 15741 Private -John Statham, of the South Lancashire Regiment, which occurred at (place not stated) on tile 19th of April, 1916, and I am to express the sympathy and regret of the Army Council at your loss. The cause of death was 'killed in action.' "

A Widow and Three Children.

Deceased was very well known in the district, and his death will be mourned by a large circle ox friends. He was 37 years of age, and leaves a widow and three children. His Army career had been a very active one, for he enlisted in November, 1914, and was drafted out to the Dardanelles in June, 1915, and was wounded in the head in August, and returned to duty in October of the same year. In civil life he was employed by Councillor J. H. Holt, builder, of Barrs Bank, and worshipped at the Congrega-

tional Church. He took a keen interest in sports, and at one time was connected with the local football club. His brother, Private Arthur Statham,. is a member of the Cheshire Regiment, and is at present "somewhere in France." His brother-in-law, Private Fred Daniels, was killed in action some time ago, and another brother- in-law, Private Fred Percival, is at present serving In Salonica. His home address is I\loB- - Newfields, Lymm.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TRIBUNAL. Farmer's Widow's Son. Ai-the last meeting of the Lymm Tribunal Mr.

T. J. Ridgway presided. • An elderly woman, the widow of a farmer, applied for an exemption certificate for her son on the ground that his services were indispen- sable to the working of the farm. She stated that her farm consisted of 90 acres of land, 50 acres of which was arable, 14 milking cows, 15 heifers, three working horses, four colts, and 10 pigs. . She also stated that she had withdrawn an appeal which she had lodged for one of her other employes. The Chairman: How many men did god keep in 1914? Applicant: Three. Have you sufficient labour if we allow you to -keep your -son?—No! I. am short. Why did you withdraw the appeal for the other man?—l was told that I could not keep the two, so I thought that if I withdrew the appeal for the other man I should stand a better chance for my son. She also stated that she. had another son at home, but he had a crippled hand, and could not milk. The Tribunal decided to grant conditional temporary exemption until rue Ist of October. "Ought To Learn." A taxi car proprietor applied for exemption for his son, aged 30, on the ground that he was his only driver, and had to work two cars, also Ithat he was their sole support. He stated that ihe did station service, and also held several contracts, including the Workhouse and Asylum. He also stated that he had another son at home, but he was only 17 years old, and was employed on munition work. The Chairman: When was this son 17? Applicant: Last August. Has he ever tried to drive the car?—No. • Why P—l think we have enough cabmen in the family, and I don't think he could manage it. as it is a big ear, and very difficult to handle. The Tribunal decided to grand conditional ex- emption.until.the Ist of Angust, remarking that they %aught -the other, son aught lo learn to drive.

LATE MR. A. B. BOUMPHREY.

Funeral At Lymm.

Born February 17th, 1857; died May 11th, 1916. Impressive scenes were witnessed at the Parish Church on Saturday afternooa, when the funeral of Mr. A. B. Boumphrey, Bayeliffe, took place, and practically the whole of Lymm turned out to pay respect to the memory of one of their most -prominent citizens. The cortege proceeded from Baycliffe to the Church, .the coffin on a wheeled bearer, being followed by several members of the family and close friends, including: Messrs. Alan Boumphrey, George Boumphrey and A. Noel Boumphrey, sons; E. J. Boumphrey and A. G. Boumphrey, -brothers; Colin Boumphrey, nephew; Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Palmer, and Sir Fred and Lady Smith (close friends), Mr. Wm. Daniels (the late Mr. Boumphrey's chauffeur), and Mr. John Driver (the late Mr. Boumphrey's head gardener). The service at the Church was conducted by the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston).' The Church organist, Mr. Wm. Cooke, and the choir were in attendance, and the hymns sung were: "Lead, Kindly Light" and "Abide With Me,"

===== 0002601_19160520_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
MINI GIRLS DEFRAUD RAILWAY

COMPANY.

Two well-dressed Lymm young women, Alice Moysten, Rush Green, and Elsie Southern, Higher-lane, were, at the Warrington Borough Court on Tuesday fined 10s. for travelling on the L. and N.-W. Railway without paying their fares, and with intent to avoid payment. Mr. Browne, prosecuting on behalf of the rail- way company, said defendants booked return tickets from Lymm to Latchford. They gave up their tickets at Latchford and came on to War- rington by train. They returned to Lymm from Warrington, but did not book, handing in at Lymm the return half from Latchford. Rail- way detectives were continually watching for these tricks, which had been going on for many years, Mr. Browne added.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
CAFE CHANTANT AND WHIST

DRIVE.

Pleasant Scenes At Wormald Green. This hot weather which we are at present enjoying makes us realise the great need for summer socks and shirts that " Tommy " so eagerly calls for, and thanks to our Rector and his wife (the Rev. and Mrs. G. B. Thurston) and a host of energetic workers, we have been able to send those gallant lads a few of these comforts. Such charities as these cannot pos- sibly go on without financial aid, and to help to swell the funds a cafe chantant and whist drive were organised and took place at Wormald Green on Wednesday. The day, was an ideal one. The sun shone beautifully, and as one passed the large and spacious grounds which were so finely decorated with bunting and caught the strains of the string band, which was continually playing pleasing melodies, one could not help but to yield to the temptation to enter and join in the merriment. Once inside, and you were fastened for the remainder of the day. You were at once besieged by scores of little children, who were selling flowers, chocolate, &c., in fact almost everything imaginable. Clock-golf was one of the principal attrac- tions, a competition organised and conducted by Mr. A. H. Evans, and creating considerable interest. It was eventually won by Mr. G. Williamson.

During the afternoon and evening concerts were held on the large lawn, and some noted artistes volunteered their services. They in- cluded: Mrs. Booth, Mrs. G. Williamson, Mrs. St. John Makin, Miss Blackburn, Mrs. Pember- ton, Miss Toft, Sergt.-Major H. Clare, Mr. H. E. Gibbon, Mr. T. Peters, Mr. G. Williamson, and Mr. Holt, of Manchester. Mrs. Garnett gave recitations, the humorist was Mr. Alfred Grounds, and the accompanist Capt. Hawkins. One of the most amusing incidents •was a needle and cotton race, specially organised for the wounded soldiers from Brookfield, who were the guests of the day. The soldiers had to engage a lady partner, who had to run with the needle and cotton to the other end of the field, where they stood, let the soldier thread the cotton through the needle, and then run back to the starting point. This created considerable amusement, and was witnessed by a large crowd. The winners were :-1, Private King and Miss M. Clare; 2, Private Tones and Miss E. Kelsal. .

The Whist Drive.

The principal attraction was a whist drive, that commenced at 7.30 p.m. inside the house. This was organised by Miss Eva HAtton, who was assisted by Mrs. Proud, Mrs. Scragg, Mr. Latham, and the Misses M. and F. Hatton. Over a hundred people took part in the tourna- ment, and the prizes were won by :—Ladies: 1, Miss Boulton ;2, Miss Boyle. Gentlemen: 1, Mr. Eric Hudson; 2, Mr. Tilzey.

, Among the other attractions and assistants were:— .

'VW, • Sports.—Messrs. T. W. F. Dixon, W. E. Hillier, IL S. Saville, jun., R. H. Gotobed, W. Thompson aid F. Giaside. • - Tennis.—Mr. T. W. 1% Dixon. String Band.—Miss Smallwood, Mrs. Cooke, Mrs. Barber, Miss F. Holt and Miss J. Holt. Sale of Flowers, &c.—Mrs. A. de C. Parmiter and Mrs. Giles Hunt. Refreshments, Teas. Suppers, &c.—Mrs. Saville, Mrs. Gotobed, Mrs. Hillier, Mrs. Holt, and, a host of waitresses. It is interesting to note that everything con- nected with the organisation. was either given or lent. There was no expense whatever.

At The Graveside.

During the service in church a large crowd of residents had assembled in the neighbour- hood of the grave. Directly after the remains had been lowered into the grave the choir sang the Psalm, "Lord, now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace," and directly after the large crowd dwindled away, having witnessed the last earthly scene connected with one of their most respected citizens.

DRINKWATER DRINKS BEER.

Knowing Expression By Chairman Of Petty Sessions. Resident of Lymm for 62 years, Thomas Drinkwater, a labourer; of Rush Green, pleaded guilty at the Petty Sessions on Wednesday to being drunk. "Is. this the first time?" inquired the Clerk of the defendant. "No!" broke in the' Chairman (Mr. A. Wat- kin) knowingly. "It is the first time for a -long time," cor- rected Drinkwater. "Yes, but I think you are very fortunate in not having been here since then. You do drink a lot of beer, don't you, Drinkwater?" questioned the Chairman, still knowingly. "Well "—Drinkwater hesitated, and then added "No, not a great deal." • Mr. Watkin : Are you going to be a little more sober in future? / Drinkwater: Yes, sir. Mr. Watkin: Your name indicates that you should be. (Laughter.) The defendant mentioned that he had three sons serving at-the Front. In fining him 2s. 6d., the Chairman remarked that they hoped he would turn over a new leaf. Drinkwater turned his money over to a police- man and then re-entered the great world with the magistrates' further injunction to be obeyed.

SAPPER PERCY CLARE.

Delighted To Return To His Old Comrades—The Cheshires.

Writing to his mother at Booth's Hill, Sapper Percy Clare, late of the R. 1., states that he has left hospital, and is expecting to return to his old comrades.

"I have just returned from hospital," he says, "and am expecting to be sent back to the boys—the Cheshires. I might have had word that I was coming home, I am that excited. It will be a treat to get back to them again. I can just fancy seeing them. Won't it be a happy reunion? Jimmy Gilberts will go mad with joy, aid by the time you receive this I hope I shall have joined them. What do you think If the new Compulsion Bill? I voice it as fine. Let those who have been making their piles of brass' come out here and do their bit. There are hundreds of young men at home who ought to be doing their bit, in fact England is alive with them,- but I am afraid they will very shbrtly be brought to their cake and milk. Then they will get at rude awakening, and begin to realise that there is a war on." • Sapper Percy Clare, it will be remembered, went out with the Lyrnm lads, but after arriving in France volunteered for mining work with the Royal Engineers. Prior to the war he was employed as a gardener at West Leigh. .

===== 0002601_19160520_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Thoughtful Moments.

Whenever we have been enjoying ourselves in this manner, there are always times when our thoughts strayed to,our poor comrades that we had been forced to leave behind, and visions of those simple wooden crosses, that mark thet- last Testing-place, ,come before our eyes, and we have seen many a:hard-hearted warrior silently brush a tear away when mention of these poor chaps has been made, for although the British "Tommy" is rough, he possesses a heart of gold.

Unwelcome Visitors.

Now the hot weather is coming on we are beginning to receive great crowds of unwelcome visitors, who stick closer to you than even your beat friqnd, and it is quite a common sight to see groups of khaki-clad warriors congregated to- gether exchanging and comparing their varioas specimens, and if our generous donors at ho -ne could only see the expressions of joy that appear on the faces of our men when they receive a new shirt or a pair of socks, which are so gener- ously sent out to the a, they would be amply rewarded, and bless the day that ever these charity schemes came into operation, which enable them to bring such joy unto the boys who have willingly gone out to do their bit. Unexpected Meeting At Home.

Sergeant Moston, it will be remembered, was forced to leave his comrades a few weeks ago owing to sickness, and was sent over to -Ireland for treatment. PRIMITIVE METHODISTS.

SuccesAful Sunday School Anniversary

The services held. on Sunday were an un- qualified success in every way. In the Rev. T. P. Mayhew the officials secured a preacher of more than ordinary ability. His addresses, both morning and evening, were greatly to the liking of his audiences. On Sunday afternoon the service 'wa.sl largely in the hands of the schelars, who, in their recitations and singing, gave great satisfaction to a crowded congrewa- tion. On Monday a large number of friends partook of an excellent tea and were afterwards entertained by the scholars, whose ef orts were received with well-merited applause. touring the course or this meeting, presided over by the Rev. R. W. Ferguson (minister) and Mr. G. L. Welford .(superintendent), an admirably compiled financial report was submitted by Mr. C. Wilkinson, which revealed the gratifying fact that the effort had realised the substantial SUM of .t3O ss. 6d., this being several pounds in excess of the result of any previous Sunday school anniversary. The scholars received un- stinted praise for the energy and resource they had displayed in their determination to make the festival a success. The thanks of the offi- cials are due, to all parties who received with such kindness and generosity their well-meant, though possibly in some cases ultra-pressing, appeals.

PADGATE.

WESLEYAN CHURCH ANNIVERSARY.

Visit Of The Rev. T. A. Turney. The Sunday school anniversary services in connection with this place were held on Sunday last, the preacher for the morning and evening being the Rev. T. A. Turney, of Liverpool. The morning discourse was taken from. Habakkuk iii. 18, "Yet I will rejoice in the Lord, I will glory in the God of my salvation." He said that our religion was a religion of joy, and it is the joyful note we want to sound most. It is easy to rejoice when things are going as we want them, but difficult when they are otherwise. Happiness does not depend on circumstances, it is a deeper thing than that. Joy has its founda- tion in the character of God. If it is rooted and grounded in Him it will be fixed sure and per- manent. The greatest calaiyities in the church are blessings in disguise. God is at work through all the events which are taking place at the 'present time. Even in our own life, when we think things are going wrong they are going right. God in the process we fail to see, though it is a very easy thing to see Him at the height of prosperity. We want more faith in God, and if we take our stand and trust Him constantly He will not fail us. Parents' Duties.

The evening text was taken from I. Kings xviii. 3, "Now Obadiah feared the Lord greatly." He spoke at length on the duty of parents towards their children, and exhorted them to get to know their children better, play in their, games and take an interest in their occupations. No matter in whatever sphere a person may be placed there is always some- thing that we can do to win souls for christ. Helpers in domestic life and Sunday school teachers have many opportunities, if they will only seize them. Keep the great aim before you as you never know what material you come in contact With. Saving the old people is a glorious work, but get hold of the children. Rescue work is grand, but preventive work is gra hder. In the afternoon a flower service was held, presided over by Mrs. Dodds, of Penketh, who addressed a few words to the children, and said that the most beautiful things in the world are children, and flowers. A very interesting ad- dress was also given to the children by the Rev. A. J. Morton.

The singing of the children was a special feature at all the services and special items and recitations were given by the primary children at the afternoon service. A solo entitled "Father, bless Thy children," was ta..stefßlly rendered by Master Douglas Picker- 'king at the'evegfaig service. The choir also ieli- dere4Staintee:What are these?" at'the morn.

PENKETH.

STRIPPED OF ITS BEAUTY.

Lady Clerk Fined For Raiding Gardens.

Seeing some flowers growing in a garden while on her way home from work, Dora Plimpton, a clerk, residing in Heath-road, Penketh, took possession of them.

The incident had a sequel at the Warrington Petty Sessions on Wednesday, when she was fined 20s. for stealing the flowers from the garden of Mr. Thomas, St. Mary's-road, Penketh. The police evidence was to the effect that on the night in question Mr. Thomas left his garden in perfect order, but on the following morning it was stripped of its beauty, tulips and other flowers being missing. Hp reported the case at the Sankey Police Station, and a constable traced the foliage from Mr. Thomas's garden to the defendant's home. Here he saw her mother, and told her that he was making inquiries respecting some flowers which had been stolen. She replied that there were no flowers there, and invited the constable to search the house. This he did, and he found the flowers in a box covered with a bag in a back bedroom. De- fendant then admitted stealing them at nine o'clock the previous night, when coming home from work.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
A MISSING TARPAULIN.

Tannery Employe Fined £5 In Railway

Prosecution.

At the Warrington Petty Sessions on Wednes- day, Rufus Plumpton, Reath-road, Penketb, was charged with stealing a tarpaulin sheet, valued £4, the property of the L. and N.-W. Railway Co., ana also with receiving same, well knowing it to have been stolen. Mr. T. S. Steel, for the defendant, said he had instructed his client to plead guilty on a charge of receiving, for a cause he would show later.

The evidence showed that on the 9th of this month the police had occasion to search the home of the prisoner, and in a back bedroom there was found the tarpaulin sheet, upon which was a number and the letters L. and N.-W. It. The police took possession of the sheet, and later on the same day Detective Hewitt, of the rail- way police, saw defendant and told him he had taken possession of the sheet, at the same time asking him to account for the possession of it. Plumpton replied that he bought it for a shilling and some odd coppers from a man who was carrying it in a sack at Penketh eight or nine months ago. The man was a stranger to him, and he did not know whether he would know him again. ' Why He Kept It.

Mr. Steel. said dilficulties did sometimes arise out of charges of receiving stolen goods. Defend- ant ought to have known that the man had no right to sell the sheet, and it was on those facts that he had told defendant to plead guilty. The defendant kept pigs, and he thought that the sheet would be useful for his pig-sty. When he bought the sheet he should have communicated with the railway company and made inquiries. Defendant, however, was frightened to speak up about the matter like a good many people were. He had never used the sheet, and if he had had any felonious intentions he could have made the sheet unrecognisable. Under the circumstances he (Mr. Steel) asked that the defendant be dealt with as leniently as possible. He had been employed at the Penketh Tannery for 16 years, and bore an excellent character. He felt his position keenly, and realised that he was in it through his own foolishness.

The magistrates thought the case was an ex- ceedingly serious one, and fined defendant £5, with the alternative of two montlrs' imprison- ment.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

ATTACK ON MOTHER-IN-

LAW.

Ironworker Gets Four Months'

Imprisonment For Wounding.

Sentence of four months' imprisonment was passed on Harry Byron, an ironworker, of 379, Winwick-road, at the Borough Police Court on Wednesday, for wounding his mother-in-law, Mrs. Mary Thompson (51), 373, Winwick-road. Prisoner had been before the Court several times previously, but, had been remanded until Mrs. Thompson was lit to put in an appearance. The Chief Constable repeated the 'facts men- tioned at the previous hearings, to the effect that at 930 on Sunday evening, April 30th, prisoner came to the house of his mother-in-law and used bad language. He was under the influence of drink, and his mother-in-law remonstrated with him. He punched and kicked her, with the re- sult that she had to be taken to the Infirmary, where it was found that she had two ribs broken besides having other injuries. Several women came into the house and ejected the prisoner. Dr. Cruickshank, senior house surgeon at the Infirmary, who examined Mrs. Thompson, stated that the fourth and sixth ribs were broken on the left side, and there were also bruises on the right side of the body and on the face. She had been under his care until 9.30 that morning, and still complained of • pains in the side. It was quite possible that some further develop- ment would take place as a result of the injuries. "A DIRTY BULLY." Mrs. Thompson said she was in the house pre- paring food for her son, when she heard some- one shouting. It proved to be the prisoner, who was bawling about some of his furniture which he alleged was in Mrs. Thompson's house. ner daughter replied that they had no furniture of his, and told her mother to come in and "have nothing to do with the dirty bully." Prisoner then rushed in and thumped and kicked Mrs. Thompson. She picked up a small jug and threw it at him. There was no provocation for the assault. Harriet Byron, wife of the prisoner, said she had not lived with the latter for a month prior to the assault. She was in the house on the Sunday. Her mother thought prisoner was going to strike her (witness) so she hit him with a slipper, and he hit her back. Witness at- tempted to shield her mother, and afterwards ran out screaming. When she arrived back she found her mother on the floor. In answer to Mr. T. S. Steel (for the prisoner) witness admitted that prisoner was a very good father and husband ;when ),..ot in drink. , She had known two glasses of beer to make him partly drunk. - - • Replying to the Chief Constable. witness said it was correct that she had left prisoner owing to his habits. She recently found him near the river with a strange woman. PSYCHOLOGY OF PRISONER.

Mr. T. S. Steel, for the defence, said the cir- cumstances were very unfortunate. Prisoner's mental and physical position was a very unfor- tunate one. He met with an accident fourteen years ago and, as a result, was subject to fits. Ten years ago he underwent an operation for the purpose of obviating the fits, but unfor- tunately it made them worse. He was exceed- ingly sorry for his wife and children. As re- garded being seen with another woman, that, unfortunately, was the failing of any epileptic person. The remarks about him being a bully, and also the fact that he was struck, might have incited him. Taking into consideration all the circumstances, perhaps the magistrates wouhl see their way only to inflict a fine. If prisoner were sent to prison and was there troubled with fits, it might prove the ruination of his health. The Chief Constable stated that prisoner had been before the Court on two previous occasion, the last time in December last, on a charge of using threats.

In- sentencing prisoner to four months' im- prisonment, the Chairman of the Bench (Mr. J. W. Hayward) said, "You will have time to think about it, a/id I hope you will be a better man in every way in the future." Several women, who went to the aid of Mrs. Thompson, were commended for their plucky actions.

"THOROUGHLY ASHAMED OF HIMSELF."

What A Sankey Man's Visit To Blackpool Led To.

• At the Borough Court on Tuesday, Wm. H. Bramley, 17, Liverpool-road, Sankey, was fined 40s. and £1 Is. specia: costs for travel- ling on the London and North-Western Railway between Blackpool and Warrington, without paying his fare, and with intent to avoid pay- ment. Defendant was not present to answer the summons, but was represented by Mr. Steel. Mr. Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the L. and N.-W. Railway Company, said defendant was a money lender. On April 18th defendant booked two single tickets from Warrington to I Blackpool, a booking clerk who knew defendant I giving them to him. At 11.20 p.m. on the same day a ticket collector named Thomas who was ion duty at the barrier, was handed a ticket by I Mrs. Bramley, while defendant said "I have only been on the platform; you were standing there when I went on. I did not get a platform ticket, but here is ld." Thomas told defendant he had come from Blackpool with his wife. The matter was reported to the railway police, and Detective-Sergeant Hewitt interviewed defend- ant,'who said it was perfectly true that he had travelled from Blackpool.

Blamed The Drink.

Mr. Steel expressed regret for the offence on defendant's behalf. Defendant's non-attend- ance at Court was due to his attending a funeral of a near relative at Birmingham. On April 18th defendant went to Blackpool to visit some•friends who were billeted there. He was alone, and when at Blackpool unfortunately got too much drink, and he did not remember whether he got a ticket for the return journey. Mr. H. Roberts: Do you contend that his wife was not with him at the time? Mr. Steel: The ticket collector must have been mistaken with some other lady. Defendant was alone.

Mr. Steel further explained that next morn- ing when defendant was sober he at once said "I have been at Blackpool." He sent a letter to the railway company expressing regret, and offering to give a donation to any charity the company might name, and pay the fare from Blackpool to Warrington. He was thoroughly ashamed of himself. Mr. Browne read a letter defendant had sent to the railway company, in which he said "1 was afraid to let my wife who returned with me know the state of affairs that foolishly lea "V 4

BLIND NOT DRAWN.

Defendant Says Shop Was Mistaken For Public-House.

At the Borough Court on Thursday, Robert Tickle, 52, Buttermarket-street, was -fined 40s. on two summonses for failing to obscure the lights at his shop on April 28th and 29th. Inspector Hesp, giving evidence as to the alleged offence on the 28th ult., said the light from defendant's shop window reflected along the street for some 150 yards. The blind was not drawn to the bottom of the window. The evidence given by the polieein support of the summons for the 29th ult. was to the effect that the blind of the shop window was up to the extent of two feet, and allowed a flood of light to reflect on the footpath. As the police entered the shop the blind was suddenly lowered six inches.

Defendant denied that there was any reflec- tion on the footpath. The Chairman (Mr. Jolley): ,Why didn't you draw the blind to the bottom?

Defendant said people came into the shop thinking it was a public-house. Mr. Jolley, in announcing the fine stated above, said defendant had had ample warning. James Lowe, 41, King-street, was fined ss. for a similar offence.

THROUGH HOUSE WALLS.

Blows On Wife Heard By Neighbours.

In applying for a separation order for Mrs. Agnes Smith against Isaac Smith, of 3, General- street, Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, told the magis- trates_at the Borough Court on Friday, that it was an exceedingly bad case of persistent cruelty. The man worked at the Longford Gas Works, and the parties were married twenty years ago last Christmas Day. During the last few months be had kicked her on the legs con- tinually, and as he thumped her the blows could be heard through the walls by neighbours. Separation order made, with 15s. per week alimony.

BROUGHT ON RHEUMATISM. Why A Workman Refused A Promotion. - Stating tilt his employers had introduced new machinery which had been. the means of reduc- ing his wages by 10s. per week, Thomas Fitz- gerald, 70, Longford-street, at the Warrington 'Munitions Court on Monday complained that his discharge certificate had been unreasonably withheld. He contended that he coal not live on the wages now paid him-30s. a week. The manager said Fitzgerald had been offered another job in the works where he could earn eightpence per day more; but had refused it. " Why?" asked the Chairman (Mr. T. J. Ridgway). "I tried it fourteen years ago," Fitzgerald replied, "and it gave me rheumatism." Certificate refused.

BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE COUNTY AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

Last week we ventured to suggest that as a result of the replay between Itylands's and West Leigh Cons., two local teams would enter the second round of the Lancashire County, Amateur Association Championship. This proved to -be the case, for on Saturday at the Wigan Subscription green, Rylands'e scored a fine victory of 224 points to. 188.

Rylands's proved quite at home on the Wigan green—one of the finest in the county—and they were 9 ahead on the first four games, Messrs. R. Tilling, J. Weave, and T. Humphreys having accounted for Messrs. J. H. Keeley, R. Sutton and A. Latham by 21-18, 21-14, and 21-14 re- spectively, but J. Langley went down 13-21. The game between J. Gleave and R. Sutton was the .best of the afternoon. On the corner to ,corner play the game was very equal, but when it came to shorter lengths, Weave showed his superiority, and ran out winner as stated.

Soon after the second four took the green things did not look quite so rosy for the Wire- men, for S. Massey was defeated by J. Lewis to the tune of 21-16. Meanwhile, however, G. Monks was "all, over" J. Littler, and when these two gentlemen left the green, the Leighite had but two points to his credit. T. Platt also won well, 21-14, and. although IL Haslam went down 16-21, the Warringtonians were 19 ahead when the second four left the green.

In the next four, T. Clare (captain) played in masterly fashion an beat W. Farrimond easily by 21-7-8, while J. Newton and W. Cottam also returned winning cards, 21-18 and 21-16 respectively. W. Roughley was defeated by J. W. Starkey 21-17. ,

The individual scores in the Rylands's v. West Leigh Cons.' match were: R. Tilling (Rylands's) 21, J. H. Keeley 18; J. Langley 13, J. Kenyon 21; J. Gleave 21, R. Sutton 14; T. Humphreys 21, A. Latham 14; S. Massey 10, J. Lewis 21; G. Monks 21, J. Littler 2; T. Platt 21,,G. Whittle 14; H. Haslara 16, J. Grundy 21; J. Newton 21, E. Winstanley 18; W. Cottam 21,.R. Newton 16; T. Clare (captain) 21, W. Farrimond 8; W. Roughley 17, J. W. Starkey 21.

A grand match should be witnessed at Wigan to-day, for two local team's, Cemetery Hotel and Rylands's, meet in the second round of the Lancashire County Amateur Association Championship. Both teams have done well to get in the second round, and will put forth every effort to come out on top.

MEMBERS WHO BROKE RULES.

The Secretary informs 114 that seVerarniem- hers of the association, having broken the rules, have. made an application to the corn-

mittee for reinstatement. After careful con- sideration the committee have decided to rein- state those who took part in local competitions other than amateur competitions, but wish it to be known that in future anyone offending the rules in a similar way will be more seriously dealt with.

THE GATE TAX.

Owing to the entertainments tax affecting the association, it has been decided to advance the price of admission-from id. to lid. to cover the tax.

SCOTSMEN ON THE GREEN.

Weekly Matches For The Davepport

Cup.

From last week onwards on the Tuesday nights of several weeks, Scotsmen are monopolis- ing the green at the Brooklands Hotel, Lovely- lane, in competing for the Davenport Cup. Mr. J. L. Davenport, it will be recollected, was the president of the Warrington Caledonian Association last year, and his benefactions in- cluded a handsome silver cup which the mem- bers competed for in a handicap on one glorious Saturday afternoon at Stretton. It has been decided that this year the handi- cap shall be extended, the play covering several weeks, and the popularity of the venture may be judged from the fact that there is a con- siderable entry. Some day the question will have to be put to them, "Does the good old English game of bowls beat golf?"

===== 0002601_19160520_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S

ASSOCIATION.

N.A. CENTRE.

The weather last week-end had a great deal to do with keeping many anglers from their favourite fisheries. We are still waiting for a trout of 13oz. to top the board, and according to reports from all quarters last week it will stand for a considerable time. Let us hope members will meet with better success this week-

end, as now the elements. for trout are quite suitable. There will be a larger, 'quantity of flies on the waters, and this should put them on the move.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOWY:

This stream is the favourite with quite a few, but results have not been up to previous years. Several trout were taken, but they were not of str.ndard size, and were returned to the water. Of course, the usual big one was hooked, but he again got the better of the angler! We could give informatioil where tackle can be obtained that would hold him and his father, but it is a trade secret, and we are not allowed to publish addresses.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
CRANAGE BROOK.

Now if anglers wished for a change, why not try Cranage Brook, Lostock ? From reports, this water has done as well as, if not better than, most waters visited. The one drawback, and it is a great one, is that trains are not con- venient for the Warrington angler, but for anglers who have cycles it is quite handy, as it is only about the same distance as the river Weaver. The top portion of the water is reserved for their own members, but there is plenty of good traut fishing for visitors, and the day tickets are 6d. Some very nice trout have been taken these last few weeks up to 11b. 2oz. All trout under 10in, must he returned to the water. Full particulars of standard sizes and where to fish is explained on the tickets. Members please note they are not allowed to fish club waters without this season's cards. There is a movement on foot to bring the headonarters' opening match forward. It meets with the approval of many. Did any member visit the Lune after the fresh? A. S.

THE NATIONAL FEDERATION.

, The National Federation of Anglers will hold its annual conference to-day (Saturday) at I Leeds. Mr. F. T. Emson, assistant secretary of , the Warrington Angling Association, and who is also a member of the .Executive Committee Fof the Provincial Anglers' AssOciation, with cwhich the Warrington Association is affiliated, will be one of the two delegates in attendance representing that strong Midland organisation. I His co-delegate is Mr. J. W. Wright, of the I Liverpool City Anglers' Association. The Pro- vincial Anglers' Association has its head- quarters in Birmingham.

ON THE BITE.

"A.0.J." writes: This, from an American poet (a-hem!) I have had by me a good while, and I am encouraged to add it to last week's sample :

Oh, we're all along the river, and we're all along the cricks, An' them as hasn't any poles is busy cutting sticks. For there ain't no use o' workin', 'cos you see it isn't right When the worms is easy diggin', an' the fish is on the bite.

Oh, you do not know the raptur' that is ou a feller's min' When he's on a log a-settin' an' a-watehin' of his line, For he's got his pipe a-goin', an' his heart is • gay an' light, An' his cares is all behin' him when the ffsh is on the bite.

Oh, the birds is singin' to him, all az happy az kin be, An' the bull-frog's close beside him, with his voice way. up in G; With his broad hat pulled down slouchways, jes for keepin' out the light, An' he doesn't hoer for nothin"cept the fish that's on the bite.

Oh, I'd ruther be a-fishin' an' a-settin' on a stump Than to be a king or emp'rer an' a-makin' people hump; For there's no sich fun az fishin' to a man whose min' is right, When the worms is easy diggin' an' the fish is on the bite.

Love's Arithmetic. Maud: Funny you should fall in love with a man 10 years your senior. Ethel: He isn't. We didn't begin to live till we knew each other, so we're the same age.

Mir

ASSOCIATION.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT MEDAL COMPETITION.

REPLAYED FINAL TIE.

WIDNES ATHLETIC v. WINWICK ROVERS.

Last Saturday the replaye'd final tip between the above clubs in the Medal Competition was played before a good crowd of spectators, on thc Atherton Quay Ground. The playing pitch was in goce condition after the recent heavy rain, and favoured a fast game. The clubs wee re2resented by the following players.-- - Widnes Athletic: Humphries; Harrison and Jim Murty; Illidge, Joe Murty and Brown; Melia, Hill, Cleaver, Broome and„Gregery. • Winwick Rovers: Riley and Sherlock; Morse, Simms and Sherlock; Edgar, HaiTison, Gleave, Gee and Anderton.- Linesmen, Messrs. Hinds and Hatton. Referee, Mr. G. Bamber. The Athletic won the toss and elected to play with a strdng wlnd in their favour. °leave set the ball rolling, and a 5n...,.rt return by Joe Murty put the Rovers on the defensive, and their goal had a lucky escape from being. cap- tured, Hollas clearing shots in -quick succession. The players received plenty of encouragement from the spectators, and some keen tackling was witnessed, Riley and Sherlock kicking strongly against the wind. The first corner-kick was taken by the Athletic, and Hill shot high over the bar. From the goal-kick the Rovers• made several determined efforts to transfer play to the athletic quarters; but their attympts at combination were easily repulsed by Harrison and Jim Murty. The Athletic forwards re- turned to the attack, and Gregory tested Hollas with a beauty, which was well cleared. Sher- lock finessing when he should have played touch, gave Hill a good opening, which he promptly accepted, scoring with a splendid shot which passed just under, the bar. After this reverse the Rover h made an attack on the Athletic goal, and -shots were sent in-. by Gee and Harrison; but Humphries was equal to the demands and cleared well. The game .was again being con- " tested in front of the Rovers' goal, and after Holies had made several good saves, Cleaver at length notched the second goal— . After thilp second reverse- the Revere mad some deter- mined assaults on the Athletie., Weave distribut- ing to his forwards very well, but Harrison and. the Bros. Murty eventually proved ,too, strong; and beat off the attacks. Joe Murty scored a third goal for the Athletic from twenty yard? range. Half-time :—Athletic, 3 goals; Rovers, nil.

On resuming, the Rovers had the wind, and were expected to be seen to better advantage, although the arrears against them were not very encouraging. The 'Athletic defence was soon in difficulties. Simms was only inches wide with a low shot. After a brief spell of even play the Athletic forwards gained a fruit- less corner. The Athletic goal was again the scene of action, and Humphries did some good work between the uprights. In the effort to effect a clearance the ball was handled inside the penalty area. Sherlock kicked the goal. A few minutes later the Rovers' defence was at fault and penalised, and Melia scored,: Hellas making a good effort to save. After this reverse' the Rovers increased their efforts, and the Athletic goal was hotly attacked. A free-kick was gained, from which Gleave scored. From now till the call of time the game was contested chiefly in the Athletic.half, and on one occasion Humphries made a grand save by. throwing him- self full length across the goalmouth to a shot from Harrison. The ensuing corner-kick was not improved upon. Final scores :—Widnes Athletic, 4 goals; Winwick Rovers, 2 goals.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT

MEDAL COMPETITION.

VOTES TO WAR FUNDS.

A meeting of the committee of the above corn- petion was hid on Tuesday night. at the Mersey Hotel, Mr. G. Bamber presiding. The Secretary reported that the replayed final tie resulted in Widnes Athletic being the win- ners by fpur goals against two. A statement of accounts was produced and all moneys found to be correct. At a previous meeting it had been decided that half of the proceeds fromthe sale of tickets should be devoted to charity. For .this object the sum of '0 7s. 4d. was realised, and it was decided that 12s. 6d. each should be given to the Mayor of Warrington and the Mayor of Widnes funds for the prisoners of war. £5 2s. was voted to the medal fund, and the medals will be pre- sented to the winners at an early date at the headquarters of the Widnes Athletic Football Club:

A vote of thanks to the Secretary for the able and , satisfactory manner in which he has con- ducted the competition brought the meeting to a close.

Some persons say that all they ask is justice. as though' justice were not the most difficult thing and the highest.—De Quincey.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
RURAL APPEALS.

Conscientious Objector Promises

To Do His Best.

ARE PUBLIC-HOUSES NECESSARY?

A conscientious objector named Stanley

Nightingale of Penketh, who appeared be- fore the Warrington District Tribunal on Wed- nesday afternoon in khaki, asked the Tribunal to grant ,him a certificate stating that he was in non-combatant service. He required the cer- tificate in case he was stopped by the military police. Mr. Mather said the young man had joined the Friends' Ambulance Unit, and he wanted a certificate to that effect to be granted him. When the applicant entered the room the Chairman said to him "You look better than the last time you were before us." We con- gratulate you." Applicant said the unit was carrying on work in France. It was understood by the Tribunal to whom he appealed that the case would be met if he joined the unit. Mr. Mather: A certificate for non-combatant service was granted. Was that qiot sufficient? Applicant: I prefer a certificate from the Tribunal.

Mr. Mather : Cannot the commanding officer give it? Applicant: No, sir; it rests with the Tribunal. Mr. Mather: I congratulate ydu on the speedy manner in which you have dropped into military custom by wearing your cap, although you are not going to fight. Mr. J. Ball: When he is in France and sees our lieople in trouble he will give us a hand. Applicant: I will do my best. The Tribunal granted the applicant the t•er- tificate asked for, and as he left the room the Chairman wished him "Good luck." Applicant: Thank you.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
FARMERS AND NURSERYMEN.

Richard Hodson, farmer, Rixton, ap- pealed for his son, aged 21, employed as a teamsman. He had only two sons on a farm of 88 acres. He could not get anyone to take his son's place.—Exemption was granted till the end of September.

A Burtonwood farmer, aged 26, was giv'en absolute exemption. Wm. Powell, nurseryman and florist, of Fearn- head, appealed on behalf of his brother, aged 33, market gardener and nurseryman. Appli- cant said his brother was indispensable to him. A number of men had left him, some had gone into munition works, others to the Army. He had replaced them as far as possible by employ- ing female labour. He had a wife and , six children.—Granted three months' exemption. Mr. Lodge, of the firm of Messrs. S. R. Lodge and Sons, sheet metal workers, of Tidal-lane, Padgate, appealed on behalf of his son, aged 21, a partner in the firm, who was the only man in cant.—Conditional exemption was granted.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
A ONE-MAN BUSINESS.

Mr. A. Browne appealed for the absolute exemption of Mr. E. Bullen (32), licensee of the Rose Inn beerhouse, Hood-lane, Sankey, on financial and domestic grounds. The appellant's wife was not capable of tarrying on the business alone, and if Bullen had to go away he would have to give the business up. Mr. Browne referred to a letter written by Mr. H. Smith, M.P., on the subject of married men, but the Chairman replied "That is only Mr. Smith's opinion."

Mr. Browne: It was given bY Mr. Walter Long in the House of Commons on Friday. He was giving directions to all Tribunals in respect of married men. The Chairman: One-man business. Mr. Browne: Yes, this is a one-man business.

Mr. Browne, continuing, said , the Tribunal knew the troubles a publican had in looking after Irishmen, 10 or 12 of whom came to Bullen's house every night. No female could regulate them. The Chairman: We don't dispute that after what has gone on in Dublin. You do surprise me to say there are a dozen Irishmen in the village. Mr. Browne: It applies equally in our country to public-houses where Irishmen go. Mr. Ball: They have no right to be here. Mr. Browne: But we cannot send them back. Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Is the business an abso- lute necessity?

Mr. Browne : The magistrates say so because they have not refused to renew the licence, which is one of the best known and oldest in the county. Mr. Browne mentioned that Mr. Bullen had two brothers. One was his (Mr: Browne's) lath clerk, Captain Bullen, who was with him a number of years. Ile had another brother in the Navy; on a submarine. Mr. Bullen employed no male assistants.

The Tribunal grantea two months' exemption.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
THE• EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 20, 1916.

THE TRIBUNALS.

EMPLOYER'S FEARS.

" Enough To Do To Get Off

Himself."

Twenty-two appeals, chiefly by married men in business, came before the Tribunal at the Town Hall on Monday, Dr. Joseph presiding.

Out of this number-, four appeals were re- fused, the remainder being given conditional or temporary exemption. SOLE SUPPORT OF HIS MOTHER.

An appeal on domestic grounds was made by it single man, aged 23, a tanner's shedman, in the employ of Messrs. S. Reynolds and Co. He said his only other brother was in the Army, joining some seven weeks ago. During that period his mother had only received 3s. 6d. per week allotment. •Appellant was the sole support of his mother, who was sixty-three years old. The Tribunal granted one month's exemption to see what was the total allotment made to appellant's mother, Captain Mathams stating that the 3s. 6d. allotment would be from the son, and the pension officer would no doubt be making inquiries. MARKET STALLHOLDER EXEMPTED.

Three months' exemption was granted a whole- sale and retail draper haying stalls in the War- rington Market and elsewhere, who appealed on business grounds. He said he had no one in his employ to manage the business.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
OBJECTED TO HOME SERVICE.

A commercial traveller, aged 35, employed by a Huddersfield firm dealing in rubber heels, tips and soles, appealed on business grounds. Alderman Tinnion: Is there no one else who can do this work? .

Appellant: We have four travellers left out of twelve, who were employed previous to the war. I have to cover a. third of one traveller's ground, and a portion of two other travellers' ground. If I went the firm would have to drop it. They have tried ladies, but they are a failure. I think I ought to be granted three months' exemption. I have been passed for home service, and I think I am just as good working in my present position than being on home service. I would rather have foreign ser- vice or nothing. Granted two months' exemption.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLOYER'S FEARS.

A joiner, aged 28, appealed on the ground that he was medically unfit for service. He said that when he attested he was passed by the doctors. He was the only joiner his employer had. He was engaged on Government work. Alderman Tinnion: Why isn't your employer appealing for you? Appellant: He is thinking about himself. (Laughter.) I heard him tell the cabinetmaker that he would have enough to do to get off him- self, without appealing for anyone else. (Re- newed laughter.) The appeal was disallowed, the appellant being advised to go before the Medical Board.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPLICATED BUSINESS.

A married man, aged 35, with a wife and four children, carrying on business as a draper, sweet stall holder, - and carrier, which appellant described as "a complicated business," was given two months' exemption, and he wan told he could appeal again.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
CINEMA HALLS AND HOME LOST.

A married man, aged 33, with an invalid wife and five children, carrying on business as an electrical engineer and. contractor, said he had been living in Warrington since July last, when he commenced an electrical business on his own account. Previous to this he had two cinema halls, which, owing to being badly hit .by the war, had to be given up. He had also to sell up his own home. He had two employes who had been rejected by the Army authorities. They were not capable of managing the business.— Two months' exemption.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTHER AND SIX CHILDREN TO SUPPORT.

A single man, aged 21, painter and glazier, appealed on domestic and business grounds. He said he was the sole support of his mother and six children. He was mantiging the business single handed, and worked overtime every night to keep the business together. Two of his employes had enlisted.—Conditional exemption was granted. BADGED, BUT APPEALED.

Appealing on behalf of a son aged 18, a mother said her husband was serving in the A.S.C. in Wales. Another son, aged 20, who had been to the Dardanelles, was now transferred to France. She had five other children. The son she appealed for was badged.—Three months' ex- emption was granted.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT ABOUT SINGLE DAIRYMEN?

A married dairyman, aged 28, who was carry- ing on the business establysned by his late father thirty years ago, appealed on business grounds. When the Chairman intimated that appellant would be given three months' exemption. appellant said "The single ones are given con- ditional exembtion." The Chairman (Dr. Joseph): Conditions have altered since then. Certain trades were auto- matically exempted, but we, have now to take them on their merits. The other decisions are subject to review:

===== 0002601_19160520_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
4,000 21b. LOAVES.

Conditional exemption It as given a married master baker, aged 33, with a wife and two children. Appellant, who was represented by Mr. T. S. Steel, said he had only one man over military age in his employ and a youth aged 16. Appellant worked 19 hours a day, and after being in the bakehouse, he had to go out delivering. He turned out 4,000 21b. loaves weekly. In addition, there was a considerable confectionery trade.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR MECHANIC TO GO.

Mr. W. J. Lake. taxi cab proprietor and motor engineer, appealed on behalf of a motor mechanic, aged 32, and married. Mr. Lake said that so far as he knew, the man was the only motor mechanic in the centre of the town. The appeal was refused. Mr. Steel represented Mr. Lake.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
GROUNDSMAN'S APPEAL REFUSED.

Mr. Steel also represented the Rylands's Recreation Club's appeal for temporary exemp- tion of the groundsman, aged 36. It was ex- plained that the man had been groundsman for the club for six years, and he had under his charge four bowling greens, cricket ground, and other recreation patches. The membership of the club was between 1,400 and 1,500, and the men were employed by Messrs. Rylands. The club had endeavoured to obtain the services of another groundsman, but had failed. It was impossible for an old man to do the work. Soldiers were also allowed free use of the recreation ground.—The appeal was refused.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
CONDITIONAL EXEMPTIONS.

Two bread bakers in the employ of Mr. T. Babbitt were granted conditional exemption. A similar decision was come to in the case of a manager and working partner in a firm of brush makers. NEED FOR CARTERS.

Messrs. A: Waring and Co. appealed on behalf of a carter aged 31. Mr. R. Higham, in supporting the appeal, said if this carter were taken it would place the firm in a very awkward position. He had encouraged recruiting and, not of a staff of 50, they had succeeded in sending twenty-five to the forces. The firm was allowing 10s. weekly to married men, and those in company houses were living rent free.—Conditional exemption Ivas given.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
CLOSED TWO SHOPS.

Messrs. B. Few and Co., bakers, butchers and grocers, appealed on behalf of the managers of the baker's and butcher's shops. They are both married. Mr. Few said the baker was the only man left in that department. If the manager of the butcher's shop were taken the shop would have to be closed. "I have already closed two butcher's shops in the town. 'They have not been let, and, therefore, the town's rates must suffer. Perhaps it is a blessing .io disguise that they are closed, as things are at present," added Mr. Few. The baker was granted conditional exemption. In the case of the butcher's shop manager, four months' exemption was given.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
EXEMPTED MEN.

Can Tribunal Compel Them To Join Volunteer Corps?

Appellants who were successful in obtaining conditional exemption from military service at the Borough Tribunal on Thursday, were re- quested by the Chairman of the Tribunal (Alderman D. Tinnion) to give a little of their spare time for the country's cause by joining the Warrington Volunteer Corps. All the suc- cessful applicants promised that they would join the Citizens' Army.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
CONDITIONAL EXEMPTION.

Mr. Westbrook, on behalf of Messrs. J. Edelsten and Son, appealed on behalf of a works manager and two mechanics, who were indis- pensable to the carrying on of the business. Mr. Westbrook explained that the firm was engaged .on important work, and the men appealed for were the only males in the firm's employ. If the men were withdrawn the firm would have to cease turning out a certain class of work. He thought the men ought to have been badged, as they came within the scope of certified occupations, inasmuch as they were engaged in the maintenance of electric plant. • In reply to Captain Mathams, Mr. Westbrook said the industry was the only one of its kind in the district. It had been in existence one hundred and twenty-nine years. In granting the men conditional exemption, the Tribunal expressed the hope that the men would join the Volunteer Corps. They wanted all the men they could get for this corps in the case of an invasion.

Mr. Westbrook said he would convey the Tribunal's views to the men.

INSTRUCTED HIS WIFE.

An insurance agent (31), married, asked for six weeks' exemption to arrange his business affairs. He said he was instructing his wife in the business. He had been working another " book " for a man who. went up last September. He had now to transfer this book." to another agent, who had been rejected.—He was given exemption till July 17th.

TRIPE DRESSING NOT WOMAN'S WORK. Thomas Lister, tripe merchant, Market-street, appealed for a tripe dresser, a married man. with five children. The employer said he had been unable to get a suitable man to fill the place of the man for whom he appealed. The Clerk : Cannot a woman dress tripe? The Appellant said it was not fit work for a woman to do. He could not send a woman round the slaughter-houses. Contain Mathams : Do von know that this man would get more by going into the Army in separation allowance than he is getting now? Does the man know. A npellant: I don't know. The application was refused. "NEED A LOT OF LOOKING AFTER."

Messrs: H. Milling and Co., provision mer- chants, appealed on behalf of their secretary, aged 35, and married. Mr. Milling said the man had been in their employ 22 years. They had lost more employes. -

The Clerk I -am glad to hear that; you are anticipating the Tribunal. Mr. Milling explained that the secretary's duties were looking after the firm's books, accounts, &c., and he thoroughly understood the rates of discounts. He had to look into bad debts, which required a lot of looking after at present. The Clerk: They always did. Captain Mathams : Especially when they are in Flanders. Conditional exemption was granted.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
MILK DEALER'S EXEMPTION.

Geoffrey Brocklehurst (38), married, with five children, and carrying on business in Norris- street as a milk dealer, was given conditional exemption. In announcing the Tribunal's/decision, Altler- man Tinnion said applicant's business was as important to the nation as it was important tp him. Applicant was a man who could do some service to his country after he had done his work. He•hoped he would join the Volunteer Corps. It might be that a milk dealer wonld be necessary to his country, and by preparin; himself and training in the Volunteer Corps he would be able to do something for the country in the case of dire need.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
INDISPENSABLE YOUTHS.

Mr. Ruddock, public analyst, appealed on behalf of an assistant aged 18, who he said had been with him ten months. Mr. Ruddock said he had a great deal of work on hand, which had to be pushed on with all speed and with accuracy. The young man was indispensable owing to the assistance he could give. The Clerk inquired whether the work could not be done by a woman, and Mr. Ruddock said it would take twelve months to train a woman.. The Chairman said the Tribunal could not see that a youth of 18 was indispensable. They had given every consideration to the appeal, and the utmost they could do, having regard to the im- portance of the work done by Mr. Ruddock, was to grant three months' exemption, That would be final, and they hoped that in the meantime Mr. Ruddock would get some intelligent boy or girl suitable for the work. It was only his im- portant work as public analyst, and on muni- tions that the Tribunal had considered the appli- cation in that way. ' DEPLETED COUNTY COURT STAFF.

Mr. W. H. Bibby, representing Mr. T. J. Ridgway, registrar of the County Court, appealed for one of the principal clerks, aged 32, and married. Mr. Bibby said the County Court staff was seriously depleted, and two men on the outside staff were ex-policemen. The whole per- manent staff was nine, and five were now in the Army. Mr. Bibby referred to the large amount of work which had fallen upon. the County Court staff, and under the Workmen's Compensation Act alone £2,000 was paid yearly through the County Court in monthly instalments. The clerk for whom he appealed had to deal with a lot of intricate forms. They were acting as trustees under the War's Compensation Act. The clerk in question was the only man in the high bailiff's office. They had been unable to get substitute, and had written to the Secretary of the County Court Clerks and Officers' Associa- tion with a view of obtaining clerks, but that official said it was impossible to get men. Conditional exemption was given. Alderman Tinnion: You are a member of the Volunteer Corns, Mr. Bibby? Mr. Bibby : Yes, and a special constable. Alderman Tinnion: You might use your per- suasive eloquence to get this man to join the Volunteers. Mr. Bibbi replied that he would approach the man on the subject. Alderman Tinnion said they di 4 not know but what they could compel a man to join the Volunteers.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE CONDITIONAL EXEMPTIONS.

A milk dealer named Walker, married. aged 32, was given conditional exemption. He said, his father was not able to look after the busi- ness. He had a brother who had attested, but he had no right hand. The Clerk: Are there ,not women doing milk rounds in -Warrington ? Appellant: I don't know of any. A master boot repairer named John WelsbY, of Bewsey-street, who anPlied for exemption on business grounds, said he had four brothers in the Army. If he ,hed to fro the business would have to be closed down.—Conditional exemption.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSURANCE CO. CLERKS.

- --- - Mr. W. E. Thornton, district manager to the Refuge Life Assurance Co., appealed on behalf of a departmental head clerk, who also 'put in an appeal asking for exemption on domestic grounds. In support of the including appeal, Mr. Thorn- ton said ten members of his staff, his own two sons, had joined the Army. Their places had been filled by thirteen female clerks. The work of the clerk be appealed for comprised the keeping of, complicate and extremely technical books. In the, district which he (Mr. Thornton) had charge of, 78 m. e.n employed by the firm had joined or were joining the Colours. They were only appealing, for district superin- tendents. Xhere were three other clerks in the Thornton office of military age. Mr. said be told these men not to attest until he told them they were essential to the carrying on of the business. Ile wonted to win the war as much as

anyone,,but they must have someone to conduct the business.

The clerk in question said he had attested. The appeal was adjourned to enable Mr. Thornton to appeal for the three other men, all the appeals to be taken together. _ .

Mr: -Thornton said he would advise the men to attest.

T.HnEE MONTHS.

Messrs. Tilling, Gray and Co., appealed on behalf of a married foreman. i representative of the firm said five out or ten eligibles had joined the Army, and one had been killed Three months' exemption was given. -

BUTCHER'S CONDITIONAL EXEMPTION. Mr J. W. Bower, 36, married, but her, 116, Buttermarket-street, appealed for exemption. Captain Mathams : Are you a Bro lA-. subject? Applicant: Yes. „ Alderman Tinnion : Were you born in England —Yes, at Newtle-m-Tyne in 1679. Was your father born in England F—No, sir. He has been here since he was 16 years old. He is now 63 years old. Is your father naturalised doubt whether he is. Conditional exemption was granted. Wm. Atherton, Manchester-road, off beer licence holder, appealed. He said he had been in the Present shop three months. Previously he had been a baker.—Granted two months' exemption. Conditional exemption was given a baker, aged 30, married, with three children, in the employ of Mr. P. Lawton, Bank Quay, who appealed for the man.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
MEN WITH SMALL BUSINESSES.

Mr. Steel supported an appeal for exemption made by Mr. W. Willcock, 35, married, painter and decorator, Manchester-road. It was stated by Mr. Steel that the appellant before the war employed six men. Two had joined the Forces. He had now two men working for him. They were in the same group as liimself, and atteste“, but he was not appealing for them. If he had to join the Army the business would have to be closed.

Two months' exemption was given, the Chair- man stating by the end of that period they would know what their instructions were ix. respect of men with small businesses.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPORTANT STONEMASON'S WORK.

Mr. J. Wood (35), married, with three chil- dren, a master stonemason, of Manchester-road, who was represented by Mr. Steel, said he was .busily engaged doing work for firms having im- portant Government contracts. He built stone cisterns for firms, and since the war broke out, owing to there being no German rods which were used in connection with the cisterns, a great deal of work had been caused in altering them. He had six to seven months' work on hand. He himself worked 90 hours per week. If the re- pairs to cisterns were not done a large number of wiredrawers would be stopped. Mr. Wood also appealed for one of the stone- masons, aged 36, married, with two children. Mr. Wood was granted conditional exemption, and the employe two months. The Chairman also pointed out to Mr. Wood the advisability of joining the Volunteer Corps. He would not only be doing good for the country, but for himself. Mr. Wood promised to join the Volunteer movement.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOP MANAGER.

Messrs. Harrop and Co., appealed on behalf of a shop manager, 35, married. A personal claim was also put in by•the man. A representative of the firm said one man who had left them had gone to work in a tannery. In reply to the Clerk, the witness said the man left a week last Saturday. He was a married man, about 40 years of age. The Clerk: Had he been in a tannery before? —I don't think so.

The representative said four of Mr. Harrop's sons had joined the Army.—Three months' exemption on the employers' claim was granted.

PLUMBER'S CLAIM.

Six months' exemption was allowed a married man (31), in the employ of Mr. E. Hough, plumber and decorator, Buttermarket-street, who appealed on business grounds for his reten- tion. The man also had an appeal in on domestic grounds.—The exemption was allowed on the employer's appeal.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLOYERS COMPLAIN

Of The Indiscriminate Calling Up Of Men.

Before proceeding with the business of the Tribunal yesterday, the Chairman (Dr. Joseph) said the Tribunal was sorry to note that general complaints had been received by the Tribunal from employers of labour that their badged and reserved workmen were being called up appar- ently without any discrimination being exer- cised. lie (the Chairman) did not know what explanation Captain Mathams might have, but he was sure he would agree that when every one was working at full pressure and under very trying circumstances, cases about which there could be no doubt should not be called up, thus entailing unnecessary work on the Tribunal and also on the employers. Captain Mathams said that was not part of his work. The calling up of the men was done from the Police Station. He understood that so many men had moved from one work to another, and from one house to another, that Colonel Mocatta considered that the only way to go through the lists satisfactorily was to call up all badged and reserved men. This was done to rectify mistakes. Colonel Mocatta was ex- tremely grateful for the ready way in which the response to the calling up had been made. If employers had been put to inconvenience he felt sure Colonel Mocatta would be willing to ex- press his indebtedness to the employers and ex- press regret. Alderman Tinnion said it should not be for- gotten that members of the Tribunal were busy men.

BANK'S CHIEF CLERK iNDISPENSABLE.

Mr. W. 11. Proud, on behalf of the Warrington Saving S Bank, appealed for the exemption of the chief clerk, aged 35, and married. Mr. Proud said the business of the bank was indis- pensable to the carrying on of the war. They were different from any other bank, inasmuch as the whole of their funds went to the Govern- ment, and would be used for carrying on the war. The bank had tens of thousands of small transactions during the year, and this entailed a great amount of work and accuracy. They must have someone who was capable of carry- ing out the duties in an efficient manner. The chief clerk had been in the bank's employ twenty years, and was a highly trained official with technical knowledge. They could not re- place the man by the employment of lady clerks. "I consider that by carrying out his duties at the bank he is helping the Government and the country in a better manner than if he was in the Army," Mr. Proud declared. Half of the staff had joined the Army, and the chief clerk was indispensable to the bank. In reply to the Chairman, Mr. Proud said it was impossible to get anyone over military age, who had had similar experience in other banks. Banking staffs have been depleted, and it was not possible for the work to be distributed among members of the remaining staff. They were already doing extra duties. The Tribunal granted conditional exemption.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
SHORT-HANDED.

Messrs. J. W. Hutchings, liowley Tannery, appealed on behalf of an assistant flesher, aged 30. A representative of the firm said they had already lost about 33 per cent, of their employes. The firm was very short-handed, and 80 per cent. of their work was for the Government. They could not get a man to replace the one for whom they annealed. They were emplo,-;ng women, and hoped to increase them. Two badged men were off ill. The firm only asked for temporary eyemntion. and the Tribunal granted exemption till the end of July.

ADJOURNED. Mews. Wyman and Co., Ltd., Winwick- street, appealed on behalf of a carter, aged .21, single. A representative of the firm said appli- cation had been made to the Ministry of Munitions for badges for a number of employes, including the man appealed for. It was agreed that the appeal should be adjourned till the decision of the Ministry of Munitions had been received, the firm being requested to let the Tribunal know as soon as received.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====

===== 0002601_19160520_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLOYES' THIRTY YEARS' SERVICE.

Messrs. Pendlebury and Co., coal merchants, appealed on behalf of a wharf manager and a traveller, both married men. Mr. Pendlebury said the remainder of the men in his employ were over military age, ranging from 74 to 47 years. The reason for this was that the men had been in his employ thirty years. The. traveller was now chiefly engaged in hunting up delayed railway trucks. The manager was given con- ditional exemption. The traveller was granted three months' exemption. A grocer, aged 36, married, with one child, was granted two months' exemption. Ile said if he had to join the forces the business would have to be closed.

Messrs. Povey and Sons, Ltd., grocers and pro.. vision merchants, appealed for the exemption of the manager of their Stockton Heath shop. It was stated that he was the only man employed at the shop.—Granted exemption till the end of August. GUARDIAN'S APPEAL FOR WORKMAN. Mr. T. Dale, potato merchant, appealed on be- half of one of his employes, aged 30. Captain Mathams said a part of the appeal gave the inference that Mr. Dale wanted the man so that he (Mr. Dale) could attend meetings of the Board of Guardians.

Mr. Dale said the Guardians met every alternate Wednesday, which was market day, and he wanted assistance for the shop.—Granted exemption till the end of June.

REFUSED!

The appeal of a single man, aged 28, a boat- man and transport worker, was refused. Appli- cant said he would appeal to the County Tribunal.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
DOMESTIC APPEAL SUCCEEDS.

Messrs. Eddowes and Co., mineral water manu- facturers, appealed on behalf of a foreman bottler, aged 35, married, with four children. The man also put in an appeal on domestic ground. Mr. Steel, who appeared for the parties, said the man had been in the firm's employ sixteen years. Four employes had joined the forces, and the firm had been unable to replace. On the domestic claim the man said his wife was a cripple, and it would be a hardship on the home if he had to go. He was granted six months' exemption on his own appeal, and the appeal of the employer was' dismissed.

===== 0002601_19160520_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
CONDITIONALLY EXEMPTED.

A master farrier, aged 36, married, with two children, who was represented hy Mr. Steel, was given conditional exemption. lie said he shod between 40 and 50 horses per week. A married salesman and carter, aged 33, ,with two children, employed by Mr. Firth Lindley, fruit merchant, was given conditional exemp- tion, as was a master plumber and carter, aged 40, who said when he was medically examined at the Barracks be was declared unfit for service. In one of two instances where appellants were given conditional exemption, the Chairman hoped they would see their way to join the Volunteer Corps. • This the appellants promised to do.

WITII THE LYMNI LADS ON AcnVE SERVICE. TOLD BY C.-S.-M. H. CLARE AND SERGT. J. H. MOSTON. WRITTEN BY S.D.M. It was in a park at Northampton, a town where we had spent a great deal of our training, when our gallant colonel, a Lymm gentleman, called for volunteers to avenge the losses that the gallant little Cheshire Regiment had sus- tained during the early stages of the war, and fully 90 per cent, of us answered the call. When we crossed over that narrow little Strip of water that divided us from the Huns, we, as Lymm lads, were over fifty strong. This took place early in February of 1915, and for a time all went well. Then we began to get casualties, and our number seemed to dwindle. Since that time we have had reinforcements, and several lads who have been home, wounded and sick, returned to us, and a right, loyal reception they got, for the reunion was a happy time for all of us. The Work of the Battalion. Of course, it is not all work at the Front. We have plenty of spare time, and the officers and N.C.O.'s always try to arrange some kind of sport, to take away the monotony of trench work. I think we can honestly say that foot- ball is the most popular of all sports. Lymm lads always did take a pride in their football abilities. Inter-platoon competitions were held regularly, and No. 10 platoon, which is a pla- toon of Lymm boys, were the cock of the bat-

REDUCED CHESHIRE EDUCATION RATE. At the annual meeting of the Cheshire Educa- tion Committee on Monday, Dr. W. Hodgson was re-elected chairman, and Canon Grant Bird vice- chairman. Mr. R. T. Richardson (chairman of the Finance Committee) said the rate for higher education this year would be 2d. in the .2, against 21d. last year, and the rate for elementary education would be Md. in the 2, which was the same amount a 5. last. year.
